The global stability of the Minkowski space-time solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations in higher dimensions
Sari Ghanem

Abstract
. This is a first in a series of papers in which we study the stability of the ( 1 + n ) (1+n) -Minkowski space-time, for n ≥ 3 n\geq 3 , solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, in both the Lorenz and harmonic gauges, associated to any arbitrary compact Lie group G G , and for arbitrary small perturbations. In this first, we prove global stability of the Minkowski space-time, ℝ 1 + n {\mathbb{R}}^{1+n} , in higher dimensions n ≥ 5 n\geq 5 (both in the interior and in the exterior); in the paper that follows, we prove exterior stability for n = 4 n=4 ; and its sequel, we prove exterior stability for n = 3 n=3 , and in all these cases, stability is studied as a solution to the fully coupled Einstein-Yang-Mills system in the Lorenz and harmonic gauges. We show here that for n ≥ 5 n\geq 5 , the ℝ 1 + n {\mathbb{R}}^{1+n} Minkowski space-time in wave coordinates is stable as solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in the Lorenz gauge on the Yang-Mills potential, for sufficiently small perturbations of the Einstein-Yang-Mills potential and metric, and leads to a global Cauchy development. We also obtain dispersive estimates in wave coordinates on the gauge invariant norm of the Yang-Mills curvature, on the Yang-Mills potential in the Lorenz gauge, and on the perturbations of the metric. In this manuscript, we detail all the material of our proof so as to provide lecture notes for Ph.D. students wanting to learn the Cauchy problem for the Einstein-Yang-Mills system.



1.
Introduction

This is a first paper, in a series of three papers where we study the non-linear stability of the Minkowski space-time solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations in
(1+n)
-dimensions, where
n\geq 3
is the number of space dimensions. In this first paper, we prove the global non-linear stability of Minkowski space-time for
n\geq 5
, however we carry out the computations with parameters that will be of use for the third paper concerning
n=3
, although these parameters will be chosen trivial both in this case of
n\geq 5
and in the case of
n=4
in the paper that follows. We also define, in this paper, the Cauchy problem for the fully coupled Einstein-Yang-Mills system in generality for
n\geq 3
, so as to refer to it in the following papers.

The problem that we look at, is that of the perturbation of the Minkowski space-time under the evolution problem in General Relativity with matter of which the governing equations are the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\cdot R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\pi\cdot T_{\mu\nu}\;,

(1.1)

where
T_{\mu\nu}
is the Yang-Mills stress-energy-momentum tensor (see (
2.5
)), prescribed by the unknown Yang-Mills curvature
F
given by (see (
2.2
)),

\displaystyle F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}}+[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}]\;,

(1.2)

where
A
is the unknown Yang-Mills potential valued in the Lie algebra
\cal G
associated to the Lie group
G
, and where
\nabla_{\alpha}
is the unknown space-time covariant derivative of Levi-Civita, prescribed by the unknown metric
{\bf g}
.

However, the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (
1.1
) imply the Yang-Mills equations (see (
2.12
)), namely

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}+[A_{\alpha},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}]=0\;.

(1.3)

Thus, the Einstein-Yang-Mills system on
(\cal M,F,{\bf g})
, is the following (see (
2.14
))

\displaystyle\begin{cases}R_{\mu\nu}=&2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>+\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;,\\
0\;\;\;\,\,=&\nabla_{\alpha}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}+[A_{\alpha},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}]\;,\\
F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=&\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}}+[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}]\;.\end{cases}

(1.4)

The Einstein-Yang-Mills equations form an overdetermined system, not any initial data set leads to a Cauchy development. The initial data set (see Subsection
3.1
), namely
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
, must satisfy the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations which arise from the Gauss-Codazzi equations (see Lemma
3.3
), as well as the Yang-Mills constraint equations (see Lemma
3.4
). The Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints for the initial data are

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathcal{R}+\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,i}\overline{k}_{j}^{\,\,\,j}-\overline{k}^{ij}\overline{k}_{ij}&=\frac{4}{(n-1)}<\overline{E}_{i},\overline{E}^{i}>+<\overline{F}_{ij},\overline{F}^{ij}>\,,\\
\overline{D}_{i}\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,j}-\overline{D}_{j}\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,i}&=2<\overline{E}_{i},{\overline{F}_{j}}^{\,i}>\,,\\
\overline{D}^{i}\overline{E}_{i}+[\overline{A}^{i},\overline{E}_{i}]&=0\,,\end{cases}

(1.5)

where
\overline{F}
is prescribed by
\overline{A}
through

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=\overline{D}_{{\alpha}}\overline{A}_{{\beta}}-\overline{D}_{{\beta}}\overline{A}_{{\alpha}}+[\overline{A}_{{\alpha}},\overline{A}_{{\beta}}]\;,

(1.6)

and where
\mathcal{R}
is given by contracting in (
3.5
). Here
\overline{D}
is defined as the Levi-Civita connection prescribed by Riemannian metric
\overline{g}
, and we raised indices with respect to the
\overline{g}
. Then, we are in fact looking for a Lorentzian metric
{\bf g}
, and therefore for
\nabla
, and for a Manifold
\cal M
, and therefore for
\hat{t}
(see Definition
3.1
), such as on
\Sigma
, we have
\overline{D}=D
(defined in (
3.4
)), and we have
\overline{k}=k
(see Definition
3.2
), and we have
\overline{A}=A
and
\overline{E}_{i}=F_{\hat{t}i}
.

Since the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations are invariant under gauge transformations (see Subsection
4.1
) and under change of system of coordinates (see Subsection
4.2
), we need to fix the system of coordinates and the gauge in order to make a precise statement on decay of the fields, which are the metric and the Yang-Mills potential. We choose to work in the Lorenz gauge (see (
4.6
)) and in wave coordinates (see (
4.7
)). The use of wave coordinates dates back to the celebrated work of Choquet-Bruhat,
[
10
]
, where she proved existence of a maximal Cauchy development for the Einstein vacuum equations for sufficiently smooth initial data. Whereas to the Lorenz gauge, it is here being used to be able to make a statement on decay for the Yang-Mills potential.

However, if one chooses to work in the Lorenz gauge, namely
\nabla^{{\alpha}}A_{{\alpha}}=0
, then the Yang-Mills equations implied by the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (see (
2.12
)), namely
\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{{\alpha}}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}=0
, imply a system of non-linear wave equations in wave coordinates, on the Yang-Mills potential (see Lemma
7.1
) with sources depending on both the Yang-Mills potential
A
and the metric
g
. Furthermore, in wave coordinates, the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (the original fields equations), imply a system of non-linear wave equations on the metric
g
(see (
7.5
)), with sources depending on the Ricci tensor
R
, that is here non-vanishing since we are treating the Einstein equations with matter, namely the Yang-Mills fields, which in its turn lead sources depending again on the Yang-Mils potential
A
and the metric
g
. In fact, since we are interested in perturbations of the Minkowski space-time, the evolution problem that we are interested in, is on one hand that for the difference
h:=g-m
, where
m
is defined to be the Minkowski metric
(-1,+1,\ldots,+1)
in wave coordinates, and on the other hand, that for the Yang-Mills potential in the Lorenz gauge
A
.

The advantage of the use of both the wave coordinates (also referred to as the harmonic gauge) and of the Lorenz gauge, is that the field equations simplify to a system of coupled non-linear hyperbolic wave equations on both the unknown Yang-Mills potential
A
and the unknown metric
h
(see Lemma
7.4
, or see Lemmas
7.2
and
7.3
).

Yet, we need to transform the initial data set
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
into an initial data of the type
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
, suitable for the considered coupled system of non-linear wave equations (given in Lemma
7.4
), so as to give a hyperbolic formulation for the Cauchy problem.

We are going to construct the initial data set
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
, considering that on one hand, the solution of the Einstein-Yang-Mills system that we are looking for is gauge invariant for both gauge transformations on
A
and for diffeomorphisms on the system of coordinates (see Section
4
), and on the other hand, in consistency with the fact that we are writing our equations in the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates conditions. Let us explain:

Given the gauge invariance of the Yang-Mills system (see Lemma
4.2
), we can choose to look at our initial data for the Yang-Mills potential to be a section of the unknown solution
A
, such that
A_{t}=0
(only for the initial data
A_{\Sigma}
), which is a condition that will not necessarily be preserved for the evolution of
A_{\Sigma}
, namely
A
. Also, given the diffeomorphism invariance of the solution (see Subsection
4.2
), we can choose which Cauchy hypersurface in the manifold
\cal M
, we would like our given initial data slice
\Sigma
to ultimately be. We choose that we would like
\Sigma
to be in
\cal M
in a way such that
\partial_{t}
is orthogonal to
\Sigma\subset\cal M
(that is a condition that will not be preserved for the evolution of
\Sigma
, namely
\Sigma_{t}
). Differently speaking, one can always make a gauge transformation on
A
, and a diffeomorphism on
\cal M
, such that the initial data satisfies the conditions
A_{t}=0
(on
\Sigma
) and
g_{ti}=0
for spatial indices (on
\Sigma
).

However, we wanted to look for a solution in both the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates. Thus, once we decided to look at our initial data set in a way that leads to
A_{\Sigma}
and
g_{\Sigma}
to be of the kind that we have just described ((
8.1
) and (
8.4
)), namely with the properties that
(A_{\Sigma})_{t}=0
and
({g_{\Sigma}})_{ti}=0
, we can then proceed forward to construct
\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma}
and
\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma}
in consistency with the Lorenz gauge and the wave coordinates condition, which is possible for us to do, because in fact
\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma}
and
\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma}
are not part of the initial data set – we just need to see what these gauges impose on
\partial A_{\Sigma}
and
\partial g_{\Sigma}
and deduce the expressions of their time partial derivatives in terms of
\overline{A}
,
\overline{E}
,
\overline{g}
, and
\overline{k}
, which are given from the initial data set (see (
8.3
) and (
8.11
)).

It is not sufficient that the “new” initial data set that we constructed, namely
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
, is in the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates – these gauges conditions will not necessarily be preserved for all time
t
, by the hyperbolic system of evolution that we deduced in (
7.4
) and (
7.13
) (in Lemma
7.4
) by making implications on the original Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
), assuming “sometimes” and in the first place that the solution will be in the Lorenz gauge and wave coordinates during the evolution. Let us explain:

The fact that we used “sometimes” the Lorenz gauge and the wave coordinates conditions, in order to simplify our original Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
), only gives us an implication on the solution (implication given in (
7.4
) and (
7.13
) in Lemma
7.4
), and this is if such a solution of (
1.4
) exists for all time
t
while being in the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates simultaneously. Now, the question is: how do we know that solving the simplified system (that we derived by using “sometimes” the Lorenz gauge and wave coordinates condition to simplify (
1.4
)) gives rise to an actual solution of the original Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
) and that is indeed in the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates for all time
t
?

In fact, we are going to show that there is indeed a way to solve the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in the hyperbolic formulation (given in Lemma
7.4
), where the evolution in time gives rise to a solution of the original Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
), that will always be in the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates for all time
t
. Let us explain how we do that:

We shall in fact show that for a solution of the simplified coupled non-linear wave equations (namely, (
7.4
) and (
7.13
) in Lemma
7.4
), the Einstein-Yang-Mills system
implies
a system of non-linear wave equations for both the Lorenz gauge and the wave coordinate gauge conditions. We will also show that for our initial data
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
, constructed in consistency with the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges, it is
precisely
the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations (given in Lemma
3.4
) that will give us that the initial conditions for the propagation (through the Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
)) of the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges are null. Thus, by starting with an initial data set
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
that satisfies the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations (given in Lemma
3.4
), we have constructed a “new” hyperbolic initial data set (in Subsections
8.1
and
8.2
) in consistency with the Lorenz and wave coordinates conditions, for our non-linear coupled wave equations (
7.4
) and (
7.13
), and we show that this will give rise to a solution for which the original Einstein-Yang-Mills system will guarantee to us that the Lorenz and wave conditions are null: the Einstein-Yang-Mills system will imply that the gauges propagate by non-linear wave equations, with null initial data precisely because on one hand, we constructed our hyperbolic initial data in consistency with the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges, and because on the other hand, we started with an initial data the solves the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints.

Thus, we have showed that if a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
) is in the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges, then it must solve the non-linear hyperbolic system in (
7.4
) and (
7.13
) (given in Lemma
7.4
), and we showed that for such a solution, the Einstein-Yang-Mills system implies that it is indeed in the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges, and therefore, that it is also a solution for the original Einstein-Yang-Mills system (in the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges). Consequently, solving the Einstein Yang-Mills system (
1.4
) in the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges, with the initial data satisfying the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints, is equivalent to solving the non-linear hyperbolic system (given in Lemma Lemma
7.4
) with an initial data constructed in (
8.1
), (
8.3
), (
8.4
), (
8.11
), in consistency with the Lorenz and wave coordinates gauges and with the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints.

In fact, in order for us to construct a solution to the Yang-Mills equations (
2.12
), that is in the Lorenz gauge for the Yang-Mills potential, given the initial data set, all what we need to do is to solve the wave equation (
7.4
), that reads the equation that we show in Lemma
8.3
. Then, Lemma
8.5
will tell to us that the original Yang-Mills equations (
2.12
) implies that the Lorenz gauge will propagate in time
t
through a non-linear wave equation (see (
8.16
)), and that the initial conditions for the propagation of the Lorenz gauge are null thanks to the fact that we started with a hyperbolic initial data that is both consistent with the Lorenz gauge and that satisfies the Yang-Mills constraints (
3.12
).

Also, in order for us to construct a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations
1.1
, that is in the wave coordinates gauge, given the initial data set, all what we need to do is to solve the wave equation on the metric (
7.13
). Now, thanks to Lemma (
8.6
), we know that the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (
1.4
) will imply, for such a solution, a system of non-linear wave equations on the wave coordinate condition (see (
8.24
)), a condition that we can write as a tensor (see Remark
8.2
and (
8.22
)). Then, Lemma
8.7
will tell us that the derivatives on the initial slice
\Sigma
of the tensor that gives the wave coordinate gauge are null because the initial data satisfies the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints (
3.10
) and (
3.11
). Also, we constructed the hyperbolic initial data in consistency with the wave coordinates gauge and therefore the zeroth derivative of the tensor that gives the wave coordinate is null. Thus, the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (
1.4
) will read exactly a non-linear wave equation on the propagation of the wave coordinates gauge, with null initial data.

Consequently, we have proved Corollary
8.1
, that gives us a way to solve the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in both the Lorenz and wave coordinate gauges, by solving non-linear coupled wave equations on both the Yang-Mills potential and on perturbations of the metric, provided an initial data set that satisfies the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints (given in Lemma
3.4
).

However, instead of working in fixed wave coordinates, we can write the equations more geometrically, by viewing them as a system of tensorial wave equations, by defining covariant derivatives with respect to the metric
m
, and not
g
, which we write as
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}
(definied in Definition
9.3
), and then look at the corresponding tensorial covariant wave operator we are interested in, which is
g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\beta}
.

This leads in the Lorenz gauge, to a coupled system of tensorial covariant hyperbolic operators, with coupled non-linear sources, where this time, the fact that we privilege wave coordinates condition is hidden in the definition of the tensorial covariant derivative
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}
. It is precisely the study of the structure of these non-linear source terms, of both
g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\beta}A_{\sigma}
and
g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\beta}h_{\mu\nu}
, that would allow us to make a statement about the dispersive estimates of the fields.

In the Lorenz gauge, we get the following system of coupled covariant tensorial wave equations on both
A
and
h
(see Lemma
11.1
), where we lower and higher indices with respect to the metric
m
, where wave coordinates are hidden in the definition of
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}
(being the covariant derivative of the Minkowski space-time, that is defined to be Minkowski in wave coordinates),

\displaystyle g^{\lambda\mu}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\lambda}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}A_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\sigma}h^{{\alpha}\mu})\cdot({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\alpha}}A_{\mu})

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\big({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{\mu}h^{\nu}_{\,\,\,\sigma}+{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\mu}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{\nu}h^{\mu}_{\,\,\,\sigma}\big)\cdot\big({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}A_{\nu}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\nu}A_{\mu}\big)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\big({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{\mu}h^{\nu}_{\,\,\,\sigma}+{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\sigma}h^{\nu\mu}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{\nu}h^{\mu}_{\,\,\,\sigma}\big)\cdot[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]

\displaystyle-\big([A_{\mu},{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{\mu}A_{\sigma}]+[A^{\mu},{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}A_{\sigma}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\sigma}A_{\mu}]+[A^{\mu},[A_{\mu},A_{\sigma}]]\big)

\displaystyle+O(h\cdot{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h\cdot{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A)+O(h\cdot{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h\cdot A^{2})+O(h\cdot A\cdot{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A)+O(h\cdot A^{3})\,,

and

\displaystyle g^{\alpha\beta}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\beta}h_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}h,{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\nu}h)+Q_{\mu\nu}({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h,{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h)+G_{\mu\nu}(h)({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h,{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h)

\displaystyle-4<{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}A_{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\beta}}A_{\mu},{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\nu}A^{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{{\beta}}A_{\nu}>

\displaystyle+m_{\mu\nu}\cdot<{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}},{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\alpha}}A^{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{{\beta}}A^{{\alpha}}>

\displaystyle-4\cdot\big(<{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}A_{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\beta}}A_{\mu},[A_{\nu},A^{{\beta}}]>+<[A_{\mu},A_{{\beta}}],{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\nu}A^{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{{\beta}}A_{\nu}>\big)

\displaystyle+m_{\mu\nu}\cdot\big(<{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}},[A^{{\alpha}},A^{{\beta}}]>+<[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}],{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{{\alpha}}A^{{\beta}}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}^{{\beta}}A^{{\alpha}}>\big)

\displaystyle-4<[A_{\mu},A_{{\beta}}],[A_{\nu},A^{{\beta}}]>+m_{\mu\nu}\cdot<[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}],[A^{{\alpha}},A^{{\beta}}]>

\displaystyle+O\big(h\cdot({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A)^{2}\big)+O\big(h\cdot A^{2}\cdot{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A\big)+O\big(h\cdot A^{4}\big)\,,

where
P
,
Q
and
G
are defined in (
7.7
), (
7.3
) and (
7.9
), and where here, the notation
O
, for the zeroth Lie derivative of the given tensors, is defined in Definition
5.2
, which is a somewhat different notation than the one we use for the Lie derivatives of these tensors in Definition
9.4
(see Remark
5.1
). As far as this notation is concerned, let us point out in this whole paper, and in the ones that follow on these mathematical problems, when we write partial derivatives, namely
\partial
, this means that we fixed already the system of coordinates to be the wave coordinates, and when we write
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}
, it is a a different way to see this geometrically, as tensors, which is sometimes useful for computation. Hence, the definition of the norms is also given along those lines, where there is either explicit, or implicit, choice of wave coordinates (see Definition
9.3
, and see (
9.15
)).

For certain systems of non-linear hyperbolic equations, such as this one at hand, one can prove that a local solution exists, under certain regularity assumptions on the initial data. One can also prove, as it is well-known, that such a local solution either exists for all time, or blows up in finite time if a higher order energy norm (such as the one defined in (
9.34
)) blows up. In other words, one has a global solution for all time if a higher order energy norm stays finite. Thus, proving the finiteness for all time of such a higher order energy norm, (
9.34
), allows one to conclude that the local solution is in fact a global one.

An important feature of this higher order energy norm is that for non-linear hyperbolic differential equations which are locally well-posed, the time dependance of this higher order energy is continuous: it depends continuously on time. Thus, one looks at the maximal time such that the local solution’s higher energy norm is bounded by a certain constant, say
C
, and if one then proves that one has actually a better bound, say
\frac{C}{2}
, then this proves that the maximal time for which the higher order energy is bounded was in fact not maximal, or differently speaking, it is in fact infinity for the time, i.e. the higher order energy is bounded indeed for all time, and therefore does not blow up. Hence, this proves that the local solution of the locally well-posed non-linear hyperbolic equation, is in fact a global solution for all time (see Subsection
9.5
). Such an argument is called a continuity argument or a bootstrap argument: one starts with an a priori estimate on the higher order energy (see Subsection
9.6
) and then one improves this a priori estimate and therefore one concludes, given the fact that the time dependance is continuous, that the a priori estimate is in fact a true estimate on this higher order energy norm.

On the top of that, if one bounds this higher order energy using a bootstrap argument, as described above, or whatever argument that works, one can then use the Klainerman-Sobolev inequality (see (
9.2
)), that tells us that if we bound a certain weighted higher order norm (if this is the norm that was being used in the bootstrap argument), then one gets also pointwise decay in time of the solution, with a decay rate that depends on the space dimension
n
of the space-time. Hence, this way, one can also get global dispersive estimates.

To effectively run such a bootstrap argument (see Subsection
9.5
), it all depends on improving the a priori estimate on this weighted higher order energy norm. For this, one has to study the non-linear structure of the source terms in the non-linear hyperbolic equation (see Lemmas
12.1
and
12.4
), as well as the structure of the wave operator itself: it is not the flat wave operator, but it is a wave operator that depends on the solution itself (see Lemma
11.1
).

More precisely, the higher order energy norm in question is a certain norm of the gradient of the Lie derivatives in the direction of the Minkowski vector fields of the local solution (see (
9.34
)). Since we are talking about a wave operator that depends on the solution itself, commuting the wave operator with the Lie derivatives in the direction of the Minkowski vector fields, gives a structure that depends on the wave operator itself and on the solution (see Lemma
15.3
). Also, such a commutation gives a quantity that depends on the Lie derivatives in the direction of the Minkowski vector fields of the source terms of the wave operator. Thus, one has to study these Lie derivatives of the source terms of the non-linear hyperbolic wave equation in order to bound the higher order energy norm.

Speaking of bounding the higher order energy norm, one applies a conservation law (see Lemma
13.2
), that is nothing else but the divergence theorem applied to suitably chosen tensors so as the boundary terms would look like the energy norm that we would like to bound (see Lemma
13.3
), however the divergence theorem generates a space-time integral that one would then need to control. This space-time integral involves the source terms of the non-linear wave equation (see Lemma
13.4
). In other words, in order to control the higher order energy norm in question (as in Lemma
16.3
), one needs to control the source terms of non-linear hyperbolic equation satisfied by the Lie derivatives of the solutions, which are nothing else but the Lie derivatives of the source terms of the original equation and the Lie derivatives of the structure of the wave equation itself which we would call the commutator term (see Lemma
15.4
).

However, in order to close the argument, one needs to improve the bound on the higher order energy without using even more higher order energy for which a bound would also be assumed – such an argument obviously does not close, as the bound on this even higher order energy could then not be improved. For this, one controls the Lie derivatives of the source terms using the fact that it is a product of Lie derivatives: one does not need to control them all, but one needs to control one factor in the product, as long as the control on that factor is good enough (see Lemmas
17.1
and
17.2
). With that control, one can then look forward to establishing a Grönwall inequality on the higher order energy norm (see Lemmas
17.3
and
17.4
).

The celebrated Grönwall lemma tells us that if the factor in the integrand is decaying fast enough, in such a way that it is integrable, then the quantity in question, which is here the higher order energy norm, will be bounded. If the initial conditions are small enough, then the bound on the energy will then be improved from what was initially assumed and used in the argument (see Lemma
17.5
). Thus, using the continuity of the growth of the energy, this would close the bootstrap argument (see Proposition
17.1
).

In the case of higher dimensions
n\geq 4
, the Klainerman-Sobolev inequality gives a pointwise decay that is fast enough to be integrable in time, and hence one could close a bootstrap argument that concludes that the higher order energy will remain bounded for all time. In the case of
n\geq 5
, using an energy estimate associated to wave equations, combined with the Klainerman-Sobolev inequality, one could get a suitable Grönwall inequality everywhere: for an energy norm defined as an integral on the whole space slice. This allows one to conclude global stability of the Minkowski space-time. In the case of
n=4
, there is a lack of integrability for a term in the interior region: inside an outgoing light cone, where one could get concentration of energy. Thus, in the case of
n=4
, one defines the energy to be only an integral on the exterior: exterior to the outgoing light cone. With this exterior notion, that we will reat in the paper that follows, one could then get an integrable factor in the Grönwall inequality and thereby conclude exterior stability of the Minkowski space-time under perturbations governed by the coupled Einstein-Yang-Mills equations.

In this paper, we will prove the following theorem.

1.1.
The statement of the theorem

Theorem 1
.

Let
n\geq 5
. Assume that we are given an initial data set
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
for (
1.4
)
. We assume that
\Sigma
is diffeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Then, there exists a global system of coordinates
(x^{1},...,x^{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for
\Sigma
. We define

\displaystyle r:=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+...+(x^{n})^{2}}\;.

(1.9)

Furthermore, we assume that the data
(\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
is smooth and asymptotically flat.

Let
\delta_{ij}
be the Kronecker symbol and let
\overline{h}_{ij}
be defined in this system of coordinates
x^{i}
, by

\displaystyle\overline{h}_{ij}:=\overline{g}_{ij}-\delta_{ij}\;.

(1.10)

We then define the weighted
L^{2}
norm on
\Sigma
, namely
\overline{{\cal E}}_{N}
, for
\gamma>0
, by

\displaystyle\overline{{\cal E}}_{N}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{|I|\leq N}\big(\|(1+r)^{1/2+\gamma+|I|}\overline{D}(\overline{D}^{I}\overline{A})\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\|(1+r)^{1/2+\gamma+|I|}\overline{D}(\overline{D}^{I}\overline{h})\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\big)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{|I|\leq N}\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\|(1+r)^{1/2+\gamma+|I|}\overline{D}(\overline{D}^{I}\overline{A_{i}})\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}+\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\|(1+r)^{1/2+\gamma+|I|}\overline{D}(\overline{D}^{I}\overline{h}_{ij})\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}\big)\;,

where the integration is taken on
\Sigma
with respect to the Lebesgue measure
dx_{1}\ldots dx_{n}
, and where
\overline{D}
is the Levi-Civita covariant derivative associated to the given Riemannian metric
\overline{g}
.

We also assume that the initial data set
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
satisfies the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations, namely

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}+\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,i}\overline{k}_{j}^{\,\,\,j}-\overline{k}^{ij}\overline{k}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(n-1)}<\overline{E}_{i},\overline{E}^{i}>

\displaystyle+<\overline{D}_{i}\overline{A}_{j}-\overline{D}_{j}\overline{A}_{i}+[\overline{A}_{i},\overline{A}_{j}],\overline{D}^{i}\overline{A}^{j}-\overline{D}^{j}\overline{A}^{i}+[\overline{A}^{i},\overline{A}^{j}]>\;,

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{i}\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,j}-\overline{D}_{j}\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<\overline{E}_{i},\overline{D}_{j}\overline{A}^{i}-\overline{D}^{i}\overline{A}_{j}+[\overline{A}_{j},\overline{A}^{i}]>\;,

\displaystyle\overline{D}^{i}\overline{E}_{i}+[\overline{A}^{i},\overline{E}_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

For any
n\geq 5
, and for any
N\geq 2\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+2
, there exists a constant
\overline{c}(N,\gamma)
depending on
N
and on
\gamma
, such that if

\displaystyle\overline{{\cal E}}_{N}\leq\overline{c}(N,\gamma)\;,

(1.13)

then there exists a solution
(\cal{M},A,g)
to the Cauchy problem for the fully coupled Einstein-Yang-Mills system (
1.4
) in the future of
\Sigma
converging to the null Yang-Mills potential and to the Minkowski space-time in the following sense: if we define the metric
m_{\mu\nu}
to be the Minkowski metric in wave coordinates
(x^{0},x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
and define
t=x^{0}
, and if we define in this system of wave coordinates

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}:=g_{\mu\nu}-m_{\mu\nu}\;,

(1.14)

then, for
\overline{h}^{1}_{ij}
and
\overline{A}_{i}
decaying sufficiently fast as exhibited in Proposition
17.1
, we have the following estimates on
h
, and on
A
in the Lorenz gauge, for the norm constructed using wave coordinates (see Subsection
9.3
), by taking the sum over all indices in wave coordinates. That there exists a constant
E(N)
, that depends on
\overline{c}(N,\gamma)
, such that for all
|I|\leq N-\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor-1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu=0}^{n}|{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu})(t,x)|+\sum_{\mu,\nu=0}^{n}|{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h_{\mu\nu})(t,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{E(N)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|r-t|)^{1+\gamma}}\;,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t>0\;,\\
\frac{E(N)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|r-t|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\;,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t<0\;,\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu=0}^{n}|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t,x)|+\sum_{\mu,\nu=0}^{n}|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h_{\mu\nu}(t,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{c(\gamma)\cdot E(N)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|r-t|)^{\gamma}}\;,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t>0\;,\\
\frac{E(N)\cdot(1+|r-t|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}}\;,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t<0\;,\end{cases}

where
Z^{I}
are the Minkowski vector fields (see Subsection
9.1
).

In particular, the gauge invariant norm on the Yang-Mills curvature decays as follows, for all
|I|\leq N-\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor-1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu,\nu=0}^{n}|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}F_{\mu\nu}(t,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\frac{E(N)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|r-t|)^{1+\gamma}}+\frac{c(\gamma)\cdot E(N)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{(n-1)}(1+|r-t|)^{2\gamma}}\;,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t>0\;,\\
\frac{E(N)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|r-t|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}+\frac{E(N)\cdot(1+|r-t|)}{(1+t+|r-t|)^{(n-1)}}\;,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t<0\;.\end{cases}

Furthermore, if one defines
w
as follows (see Definition
9.2
),

\displaystyle w(q):=\begin{cases}(1+|r-t|)^{1+2\gamma}\quad\text{when }\quad r-t>0\;,\\
1\,\quad\text{when }\quad r-t<0\;,\end{cases}

(1.18)

and if we define
\Sigma_{t}
as being the time evolution in wave coordinates of
\Sigma
, then for all time
t
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\sum_{|J|\leq N}\big(\|w^{1/2}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J}}h(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{t})}+\|w^{1/2}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J}}A(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{2}\Sigma_{t})}\big)

(1.19)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle E(N)\;.

More precisely, for any constant
E(N)
, there exist two constants, a constant
c_{1}
that depends on
\gamma>0
and on
n\geq 5
, and a constant
c_{2}
(to bound
\overline{{\cal E}}_{N}(0)
defined in (
1
)), that depends on
E(N)
, on
N\geq 2\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+2
and on
w
(i.e. depends on
\gamma
), such that if

\displaystyle\overline{{\cal E}}_{(\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)}(0)\leq c_{1}(\gamma,n)\;,

(1.20)

and if

\displaystyle\overline{{\cal E}}_{N}(0)\leq c_{2}(E(N),N,\gamma)\;,

(1.21)

then, we have for all time
t
,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\;.

(1.22)

2.
The Einstein-Yang-Mills equations

2.1.
The set-up

We consider that we are given an arbitrary compact Lie group
G
, and a positive definite Ad-invariant scalar product,
<

,

>
, on the Lie algebra
\cal G
, associated to the Lie group
G
.

The unknowns that we are looking for are
(\cal{M},A,{\bf g})
, where
\cal M
is an unknown manifold, where
A
is an unknown Yang-Mills potential, which in a given system of coordinates
x^{\alpha}
, is a one-form
A
on the manifold
\cal M
, valued in the Lie algebra
\cal G
, and can be written as

A=A_{\alpha}dx^{\alpha}\,,

and where
{\bf g}
is an unknown Lorentzian metric.

Let
\nabla_{\alpha}
be the Levi-Civita covariant derivative, that is a torsion free connection and compatible with the metric
{\bf g}
that is part of the unknowns
(\cal{M},A,{\bf g})
that we are looking for. We define the gauge covariant derivative of any arbitrary tensor
\Psi
valued in the Lie algebra
\cal G
, as

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\alpha}\Psi:=\nabla_{\alpha}\Psi+[A_{\alpha},\Psi]\,.

(2.1)

The Yang-Mills curvature,
F
, is a two-form defined by

\displaystyle F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}}+[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}]\,.

(2.2)

2.2.
The field equations

In a given system of coordinates, we define

\displaystyle e_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\mu}}\,.

Let
R_{{\alpha}\beta\gamma\delta}
be the Riemann tensor that is

\displaystyle R_{{\alpha}\beta\gamma\delta}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle g(e_{\alpha},\nabla_{e_{\gamma}}\nabla_{e_{\delta}}e_{\beta}-\nabla_{e_{\delta}}\nabla_{e_{\gamma}}e_{\beta}-\nabla_{[e_{\gamma},e_{\delta}]}e_{\beta})\,.

(2.3)

We are in fact looking for a (1+
n
)-dimensional globally hyperbolic Lorentzian manifold
(\cal M,{\bf g})
, and a one-form
A
defined on this manifold, which satisfy the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, which are

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\pi\cdot T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(2.4)

where

\displaystyle T_{\mu\nu}=\frac{1}{4\pi}\cdot(<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>)\,,

(2.5)

where
R_{\mu\nu}
is the Ricci tensor, that is

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}:=R^{\alpha}_{\,\,\,\mu\alpha\nu}:=g^{{\alpha}\sigma}R_{\sigma\mu\alpha\nu}\,,

(2.6)

and where the scalar of Ricci
R
is given by

\displaystyle R:=R_{\mu}^{\,\;\;\mu}:=g^{{\alpha}\sigma}R_{\mu\sigma}\,.

(2.7)

Here, we have used the Einstein summation convention of lowering and highering indices with respect to the unknown background metric
{\bf g}
.

However, the expression of
F
in terms of
A
, (
2.2
), leads to the Bianchi identities for the Yang-Mills curvature (see
[
24
]
),

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{{\alpha}}F_{\mu\nu}+\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\mu}F_{\nu{\alpha}}+\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\nu}F_{{\alpha}\mu}=0\,.

(2.8)

Since
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita covariant derivative, we have the Bianchi identities for the Riemann tensor

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\alpha}}R^{\gamma}_{\,\,\,{\beta}\mu\nu}+\nabla_{\mu}R^{\gamma}_{\,\,\,{\beta}\nu{\alpha}}+\nabla_{\nu}R^{\gamma}_{\,\,\,{\beta}{\alpha}\mu}=0\,.

(2.9)

Contracting, we get

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\alpha}}R^{{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\beta}\mu\nu}+\nabla_{\mu}R^{{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\beta}\nu{\alpha}}+\nabla_{\nu}R^{{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\beta}{\alpha}\mu}=\nabla_{{\alpha}}R^{{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\beta}\mu\nu}-\nabla_{\mu}R_{{\beta}\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}R_{{\beta}\mu}=0\,.

Contracting again, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}R_{{\alpha}{\beta}\mu}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;\;\;\;{\beta}}-\nabla_{\mu}R_{{\beta}}^{\,\;\;{\beta}}+\nabla^{{\beta}}R_{{\beta}\mu}=0\,,

which leads to

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}R_{{\alpha}\mu}-\nabla_{\mu}R+\nabla^{{\beta}}R_{{\beta}\mu}=0\,,

and hence

\displaystyle 2(\nabla^{{\alpha}}R_{{\alpha}\mu}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{\mu}R)=0\,.

(2.10)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}(R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}{\bf g}_{\mu\nu}R)=0\,,

which in its turn implies that

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}=0\,.

(2.11)

Using the Bianchi identities for the Yang-Mills curvature (
2.8
), the fact that
<

,

>
is Ad-invariant, that the connection
\nabla
is compatible with the metric
{\bf g}
, then the fact that the energy-momentum tensor is divergence free, (
2.11
), leads to the following Yang-Mills equation (see
[
24
]
),

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{{\alpha}}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}:=\nabla_{\alpha}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}+[A_{\alpha},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}]=0\;.

(2.12)

Now, contracting the left hand side of the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, (
2.4
), gives

\displaystyle{R_{\mu}}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}{g_{\mu}}^{\mu}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{R_{\mu}}^{\mu}-\frac{1}{2}g^{\mu{\alpha}}g_{\mu{\alpha}}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R-\frac{(n+1)}{2}R=\frac{(1-n)}{2}R\,.

Thus, the full contraction of the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations leads to

\displaystyle\frac{(1-n)}{2}\cdot R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\pi\cdot{T_{\mu}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\pi\cdot\frac{1}{4\pi}(<F_{\mu{\beta}},F^{\mu{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}{g_{\mu}}^{\mu}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>-\frac{(n+1)}{4}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot\frac{(3-n)}{4}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(3-n)}{2}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>.

Therefore,

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>.

(2.13)

Consequently, the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations in (1+
n
)-dimensions, (
2.4
), can be written as

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{2}g_{\mu\nu}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\,,

which yields to

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>+g_{\mu\nu}\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;.

(2.14)

Finally, the Einstein Yang-Mills equations are given by the following system

\displaystyle\begin{cases}R_{\mu\nu}=&2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>+\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;,\\
0\;\;\;\,\,=&\nabla_{\alpha}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}+[A_{\alpha},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}]\;,\\
F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=&\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}}+[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}]\;.\end{cases}

(2.15)

3.
The Cauchy problem and the constraints for the Einstein-Yang-Mills system

3.1.
The Cauchy problem

Definition 3.1
.

Since the unknown space-time
(\cal{M},{\bf g})
is globally hyperbolic, we know by then that there exists a smooth vector field
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
such that
\cal{M}
is foliated by Cauchy hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
. The one-form
(dt)_{\mu}
defines a vector field
g^{\mu\nu}(dt)_{\nu}
orthogonal to the hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
. This vector field could then in turn be normalised to define a unit timelike vector
\hat{t}
orthogonal to
\Sigma_{t}
.

In fact, let

\displaystyle N=\big(-(dt)^{\mu}(dt)_{\mu}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\big(-g_{\mu\nu}(dt)^{\mu}(dt)^{\nu}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\;.

(3.1)

Then, at each point
p
on
\Sigma_{t}
, we define

\displaystyle\hat{t}^{\nu}=\frac{1}{N}(dt)^{\nu}\;.

(3.2)

Definition 3.2
.

For
U,V
vector fields tangent to
\Sigma_{t}
, let second fundamental form
k
be defined by

\displaystyle k(U,V)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{U}\hat{t},V)\;.

(3.3)

We are looking for an unknown (1+
n
)-dimensional globally hyperbolic manifold
(\cal M,{\bf g})
, therefore foliated by space-like hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
, where
t
is a smooth time function, and
\hat{t}
is a timelike vector orthogonal to
\Sigma_{t}
(defined in Definition
3.1
), and we are looking for an unknown Yang-Mills curvature
F
on
\cal M
, which solve the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (
2.15
) on
(\cal M,F,{\bf g})
. The Cauchy problem for the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations can be formulated as follows:

We consider that we are given an initial data set for the space-time, that is
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
, which consists of an
n
-dimensional manifold
\Sigma
with a Riemannian metric
{\overline{g}}
, and a symmetric two-tensor
k_{0}
, and consists of an initial data for the Yang-Mills fields which are two one-tensors
{\overline{A}}={\overline{A}}_{i}dx^{i}
and
E=E_{i}dx^{i}
on
\Sigma
valued in the Lie algebra
\cal G
. We are then looking for a (1+
n
)-dimensional Lorentzian manifold
\cal M
, with Yang-Mills curvature
F
, which solve the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (
2.15
), such that
\Sigma=\Sigma_{t_{0}}\subset\cal M
, and such that
{\overline{g}}
is the restriction of
{\bf g}
on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}\subset\cal M
, and
\overline{k}
is the restriction of the second fundamental form
k
on
\Sigma_{t_{0}}\subset\cal M
(defined in Definition
3.2
in (
3.3
)), and such that
E_{i}=F_{\hat{t}i}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In this series of papers, we are going to impose that
\{t,x_{1},...,x_{n}\}
satisfy the wave coordinate condition (see Section
4
), we are going to construct the Minkowski metric using this wave coordinates system (see Section
5
), and we are going to impose that
A
satisfies the Lorenz condition (see Section
4
). We are going to construct
t
such that
t=0
on
\Sigma\subset\cal M
. We also use the notation
x^{0}=t
.

3.2.
The constraint equations

The Einstein-Yang-Mills equations are overdetermined – not any initial data set,
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
, for the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations (
2.15
), leads to a Cauchy development. In fact, the initial data must satisfy itself the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations. Let us explain in what follows.

Definition 3.3
.

For
U,V
vector fields tangent to
\Sigma_{t}
, let

\displaystyle D_{U}V

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\nabla_{U}V-k(U,V)\,\hat{t}\;,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\ bcd}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle g(e^{a},D_{e_{c}}D_{e_{d}}e_{b}-D_{e_{d}}D_{e_{c}}e_{b}-D_{[e_{c},e_{d}]}e_{b})\;.

(3.5)

We will show the following well-known lemmas.

Lemma 3.1
.

The tensor
k
, the second fundamental form of the hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
, is symmetric, that is for all
U,V\in T\Sigma_{t}
, we have
k(U,V)=k(V,U)
.

Proof.

We know that for all
U\in T\Sigma_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle g(\hat{t},U)=0\,.

This along with the fact that
\nabla_{V}g(\hat{t},U)=0
, we obtain

\displaystyle 0=V(g(\hat{t},U))=g(\nabla_{V}\hat{t},U)+g(\hat{t},\nabla_{V}U)\,.

Now, for all
U,V\in T\Sigma_{t}
, we have
[U,V]:=UV-VU\in T\Sigma_{t}
. Thus,

\displaystyle 0=g(\hat{t},[U,V])=g(\hat{t},\nabla_{U}V-\nabla_{V}U)

(since the metric is torsion free). Consequently,

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{V}\hat{t},U)=-g(\hat{t},\nabla_{V}U)=-g(\hat{t},\nabla_{U}V)\,.

However, since
0=U(g(\hat{t},V))
, we get

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{V}\hat{t},U)=g(\nabla_{U}\hat{t},V)\,.

Lemma 3.2
.

The connection
D
is compatible with the metric
{\bf g}
, that is
Dg=0
.

Proof.

Since the metric
g
is compatible with the metric, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{c}g(e_{a},e_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{c}e_{a},e_{b})+g(e_{a},\nabla_{c}e_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(D_{c}e_{a}+k_{ca}\hat{t},e_{b})+g(e_{a},D_{c}e_{b}+k_{cb}\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(D_{c}e_{a},e_{b})+g(e_{a},D_{c}e_{b})

(since
\hat{t}
is orthogonal to
\Sigma_{t})
.

Lemma 3.3
.

We have the Gauss-Codazzi equations which say that for a spatial frame
\{e_{a},e_{b},e_{c}\}
tangent to the hypersurface
\Sigma_{t}
, we have

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}-k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}k_{bc}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,c}k_{bd}\;,

(3.6)

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{e_{c}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}-D_{e_{d}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,c}\;.

(3.7)

Proof.

The Gauss equations:

We will show the well-known proof of the following Gauss equations

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}-k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}k_{bc}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,c}k_{bd}\;.

In fact, we have,

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\nabla_{e_{d}}e_{b}-\nabla_{e_{d}}\nabla_{e_{c}}e_{b}-\nabla_{[e_{c},e_{d}]}e_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\nabla_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\nabla_{e_{c}}e_{b})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(\nabla_{c}e_{d}-\nabla_{d}e_{c})}e_{b})

(since
\nabla
is a Levi-Civita connection and therefore torsion free)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g(\nabla_{e_{c}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}e_{b})-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}e_{b})

\displaystyle+g(\nabla_{e_{d}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}e_{b})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(\nabla_{c}e_{d}-\nabla_{d}e_{c})}e_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}g(e^{a},D_{e_{d}}e_{b}+k_{db}\hat{t})-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a}+{k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{d}}e_{b}+k_{db}\hat{t})-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},D_{e_{c}}e_{b}+k_{cb}\hat{t})

\displaystyle+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a}+{k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{c}}e_{b}+k_{cb}\hat{t})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{c}e_{d}+k_{cd}\hat{t}-D_{d}e_{c}-k_{dc}\hat{t})}e_{b})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}g(e^{a},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a}+{k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{d}}e_{b}+k_{db}\hat{t})-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})

\displaystyle+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a}+{k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{c}}e_{b}+k_{cb}\hat{t})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{c}e_{d}+k_{cd}\hat{t}-D_{d}e_{c}-k_{dc}\hat{t})}e_{b})

(where we used the fact that
\hat{t}
is orthogonal to
\Sigma_{t}
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})+g(e^{a},D_{e_{c}}D_{e_{d}}e_{b})

\displaystyle-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},k_{db}\hat{t})-g({k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g({k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},k_{db}\hat{t})

\displaystyle-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})

\displaystyle+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},k_{cb}\hat{t})+g({k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})+g({k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},k_{cb}\hat{t})

\displaystyle-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{c}e_{d}-D_{d}e_{c})}e_{b})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(k_{cd}\hat{t}-k_{dc}\hat{t})}e_{b})

(where we used the fact that also
D
is compatible with the metric
g
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})+g(e^{a},D_{e_{c}}D_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g(e^{a},D_{e_{d}}D_{e_{c}}e_{b})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{c}e_{d}-D_{d}e_{c})}e_{b})

\displaystyle-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},k_{db}\hat{t})-g({k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})-g({k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},k_{db}\hat{t})

\displaystyle-g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})

\displaystyle+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},k_{cb}\hat{t})+g({k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})+g({k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},k_{cb}\hat{t})

(where we used the fact that
k
is symmetric)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\ bcd}-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},k_{db}\hat{t})-g({k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{d}}e_{b})+{k_{c}}^{a}k_{db}

\displaystyle+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},k_{cb}\hat{t})+g({k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},D_{e_{c}}e_{b})-{k_{d}}^{a}k_{cb}\,.

However, we have for
U,V
tangent to
\Sigma_{t}

\displaystyle D_{U}V

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{U}V-k(U,V)\hat{t}\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle g(D_{U}V,\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{U}V,\hat{t})-k(U,V)g(\hat{t},\hat{t})

(3.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-k(U,V)-k(U,V)g(\hat{t},\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-k(U,V)+k(U,V)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,.

Hence,

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\,\,\,bcd}+k^{\,\,\,a}_{c}k_{bd}-k^{\,\,\,a}_{d}k_{bc}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\ bcd}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,c}k_{bd}-k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}k_{bc}

(using the symmetry of the second fundamental form
k
).

The Codazzi equations

Now, we prove the Codazzi equations

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{e_{c}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}-D_{e_{d}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,c}\;.

We have,

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t}-\nabla_{e_{d}}\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t}-\nabla_{[e_{c},e_{d}]}\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{[e_{c},e_{d}]}\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-g(\nabla_{e_{c}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})

\displaystyle+g(\nabla_{e_{d}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(\nabla_{e_{c}}e_{d}-\nabla_{e_{d}}e_{c})}\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-g(\nabla_{e_{c}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})

\displaystyle+g(\nabla_{e_{d}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}+k_{cd}\hat{t}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c}-k_{dc}\hat{t})}\hat{t})\,.

However, we have

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{{\alpha}}}\hat{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(\nabla_{e_{{\alpha}}}\hat{t},e_{\mu})e^{\mu}-g(\nabla_{e_{{\alpha}}}\hat{t},\hat{t})\hat{t}

(3.9)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle k_{{\alpha}\mu}e^{\mu}

(where we used the fact that
\hat{t}
is a unit vector field).

Thus,

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-g(\nabla_{e_{c}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{d}}\hat{t})-\partial_{e_{d}}g(e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})+g(\nabla_{e_{d}}e^{a},\nabla_{e_{c}}\hat{t})

\displaystyle-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}+k_{cd}\hat{t}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c}-k_{dc}\hat{t})}\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}{k^{a}}_{d}-\partial_{e_{d}}{k^{a}}_{c}-g(\nabla_{e_{c}}e^{a},k_{d\mu}e^{\mu})+g(\nabla_{e_{d}}e^{a},k_{c\mu}e^{\mu})

\displaystyle-g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c})}\hat{t})

(using the symmetry of
k
).

Yet,

\displaystyle g(e^{a},\nabla_{(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c})}\hat{t})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(e^{a},k_{\nu\mu}(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c})^{\nu}e^{\mu})=k_{\nu a}(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c})^{\nu}\,,

\displaystyle\nabla_{e_{c}}e^{a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{e_{c}}e^{a}+k_{c}^{\,\,\,a}\hat{t}\,.

Hence,

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}{k^{a}}_{d}-\partial_{e_{d}}{k^{a}}_{c}-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a}+{k_{c}}^{a}\hat{t},k_{d\mu}e^{\mu})+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a}+{k_{d}}^{a}\hat{t},k_{c\mu}e^{\mu})

\displaystyle-k_{\nu a}(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c})^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}{k^{a}}_{d}-\partial_{e_{d}}{k^{a}}_{c}-g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},k_{d\mu}e^{\mu})+g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},k_{c\mu}e^{\mu})

\displaystyle-{k^{a}}_{\nu}(D_{e_{c}}e_{d}-D_{e_{d}}e_{c})^{\nu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}{k^{a}}_{d}-k_{d\mu}g(D_{e_{c}}e^{a},e^{\mu})-{k^{a}}_{\nu}(D_{e_{c}}e_{d})^{\nu}-\partial_{e_{d}}{k^{a}}_{c}+{k^{a}}_{\nu}(D_{e_{d}}e_{c})^{\nu}+k_{c\mu}g(D_{e_{d}}e^{a},e^{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{e_{c}}{k^{a}}_{d}-k_{\mu d}(D_{e_{c}}e^{a})^{\mu}-{k^{a}}_{\nu}(D_{e_{c}}e_{d})^{\nu}-\partial_{e_{d}}{k^{a}}_{c}+{k^{a}}_{\nu}(D_{e_{d}}e_{c})^{\nu}+k_{\mu c}(D_{e_{d}}e^{a})^{\mu}

(where we used the symmetry of
k
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{e_{c}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}-D_{e_{d}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,c}\,.

Lemma 3.4
.

The constraint equations for the Einstein-Yang-Mills system are

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}k_{c}^{\,\,\,c}-k^{ac}k_{ac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(n-1)}<E_{b},E^{b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>\,,

(3.10)

\displaystyle D_{e_{a}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}-D_{e_{d}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<E_{b},{F_{d}}^{\,b}>\,,

(3.11)

\displaystyle D^{i}E_{i}+[A^{i},E_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,,

(3.12)

where
E_{i}=F_{\hat{t}i}
, and where the summation is carried only over spatial indices. Here
\hat{t}
,
k
,
D
and
\mathcal{R}
are given in Definitions
3.1
,
3.2
, and
3.3
.

Proof.

Based on Lemma
3.3
, and by raising indices, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{\,\,\,ba}^{a\,\,\;\;\;d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{d\mu}R^{a}_{\,\,\,ba\mu}=g^{dc}R^{a}_{\,\,\,bac}={\mathcal{R}^{a}_{\,\,\,ba}}^{d}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}k_{b}^{\,\,\,d}-k^{ad}k_{ba}

(where we used the fact that
\hat{t}
is orthogonal to the Cauchy hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
).

Thus, summing over spatial indices, we obtain

\displaystyle R_{\,\,\,ba}^{a\,\,\;\;\;b}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{\,\,\,ba}^{a\,\,\;\;\;b}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}k_{b}^{\,\,\,b}-k^{ab}k_{ba}\,.

But,

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{\mu}^{\,\,\;\mu}=g^{\nu\mu}R_{\mu\nu}=g^{\nu\mu}R_{\mu{\alpha}\nu}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\;\;\;{\alpha}}=g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{\hat{t}{\alpha}\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}+g^{ab}R_{a{\alpha}b}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}=g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}{R_{\hat{t}a\hat{t}}}^{a}+g^{ab}g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}{R_{a{\alpha}b{\beta}}}

(where we used the symmetries of the Riemann tensor)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{\hat{t}a\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+g^{ab}g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}{R_{a\hat{t}b\hat{t}}}+g^{ab}g^{cd}{R_{acbd}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{\hat{t}a\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{a\hat{t}\,\,\,\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+g^{ab}g^{cd}{R_{acbd}}=g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{\hat{t}a\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{a\hat{t}\,\,\,\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}

(where we used again the fact that
\hat{t}
is orthogonal to the Cauchy hypersurfaces
\Sigma_{t}
)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}R_{\hat{t}a\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}=-2R_{\hat{t}a\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,a}+R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}=-2R_{\hat{t}{\alpha}\hat{t}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}+R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}=-2R_{\hat{t}\hat{t}}+R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}

(where we used the symmetries of the Riemann tensor)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2R_{\hat{t}\hat{t}}+R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}\,.

However, since
R=\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>+2R_{\hat{t}\hat{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}\,,

(3.13)

and hence,

\displaystyle R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2R_{\hat{t}\hat{t}}+\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>=2(8\pi T_{\hat{t}\hat{t}})+\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2[2\big(<F_{\hat{t}{\beta}},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}g_{\hat{t}\hat{t}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\big)]+\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4<F_{\hat{t}{\beta}},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{{\beta}}>+<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>+\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4<F_{\hat{t}{\beta}},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{{\beta}}>+\frac{2(2-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\,.

However,

\displaystyle<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<F_{\hat{t}{\beta}},F^{\hat{t}{\beta}}>+<F_{a{\beta}},F^{a{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<F_{\hat{t}b},F^{\hat{t}b}>+<F_{a{\beta}},F^{a{\beta}}>=<F_{\hat{t}b},F^{\hat{t}b}>+<F_{a\hat{t}},F^{a\hat{t}}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>

(using the anti-symmetry of the Yang-Mills curvature)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<F_{\hat{t}b},F^{\hat{t}b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>=2g^{\hat{t}\mu}<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\mu}}^{\,b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2g^{\hat{t}\hat{t}}<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>=-2<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>

(where we used the fact that
\hat{t}
is a unit-orthogonal vector to the

Cauchy hypersurfaces foliation).

Thus,

\displaystyle R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4<F_{\hat{t}{\beta}},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,{\beta}}>+\frac{2(2-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>-\frac{4(2-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>+\frac{2(2-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{ab},F^{ab}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(n-1)}<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>\,.

However, Gauss equations give

\displaystyle R_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}-k^{ac}k_{ca}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}k_{c}^{\,\,\,c}\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{ac}^{\,\,\,\,\,\;ac}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}k_{c}^{\,\,\,c}-k^{ac}k_{ac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(n-1)}<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>\,.

Now, we look at

\displaystyle R^{a}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}ad}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R^{\mu}_{\,\,\,\hat{t}\mu d}=R_{\hat{t}d}

(3.15)

(using the symmetries of the Riemann curvature)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\pi T_{\hat{t}d}+\frac{1}{2}g_{\hat{t}d}\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2(<F_{\hat{t}{\beta}},{F_{d}}^{\,{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}g_{\hat{t}d}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>)+\frac{1}{2}g_{\hat{t}d}\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{d}}^{\,b}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{e_{a}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}-D_{e_{d}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}\,.

Hence, the constraint equations for the Einstein-Yang-Mills system are

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}+k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}k_{c}^{\,\,\,c}-k^{ac}k_{ac}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(n-1)}<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{\hat{t}}}^{\,b}>+<F_{ab},F^{ab}>\,,

(3.16)

\displaystyle D_{e_{a}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,d}-D_{e_{d}}k^{a}_{\,\,\,a}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<F_{\hat{t}b},{F_{d}}^{\,b}>\,.

(3.17)

Also, since we want
E_{i}=F_{\hat{t}i}
on
\Sigma
, then in view of the fact that
\textbf{D}^{(A)\,i}F_{\hat{t}i}=0
(which is implied from the Einstein-Yang-Mills system), we get

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(A)\,i}F_{\hat{t}i}=\textbf{D}^{(A)\,i}E_{i}=\nabla^{i}E_{i}+[A^{i},E_{i}]=0\,.

(3.18)

4.
The gauges invariance of the equations and fixing the gauges

The Einstein-Yang-Mills equations are invariant under both gauge transformations and diffeomorphisms. We explain in what follows.

4.1.
The invariance under gauge transformation

For any Yang-Mills potential
A_{\alpha}
solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system and for any element
\cal{O}\in G
, since
G
is a Lie group and therefore a group, there exists an inverse
\cal{O}^{-1}\in G
, and therefore we can define

\displaystyle\tilde{A_{\alpha}}=\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\,,

(4.1)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}}=\nabla_{{\alpha}}\tilde{A_{{\beta}}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}\tilde{A_{{\alpha}}}+[\tilde{A_{{\alpha}}},\tilde{A_{{\beta}}}]\,.

(4.2)

We have the following well-known lemma:

Lemma 4.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\tilde{F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}}=\cal{O}F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}\,,

and for any tensor
\Psi
valued in the Lie algebra
\cal G
associated to the Lie group
G
, if

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}=\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}\,,

then,

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{\alpha}\tilde{\Psi}=\cal{O}(\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\alpha}\Psi)\cal{O}^{-1}\,.

Proof.

Computing

\displaystyle\tilde{F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\alpha}}\tilde{A_{{\beta}}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}\tilde{A_{{\alpha}}}+[\tilde{A_{{\alpha}}},\tilde{A_{{\beta}}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\alpha}}(\cal{O}A_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})-\nabla_{{\alpha}}\big((\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\big)-\nabla_{{\beta}}(\cal{O}A_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})+\nabla_{{\beta}}\big((\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle+[\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1},\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O})A_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}})\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}A_{{\beta}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O})A_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}(\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}})\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{{\beta}}\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle+[\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1},\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}]-[\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1},(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}]-[(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1},\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}]

\displaystyle+[(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1},(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}]\,,

and developing the terms in the commutators, we get

\displaystyle\tilde{F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}})\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O})A_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}A_{{\beta}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O})A_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{{\beta}}\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-\big(\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle-\big((\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle+\big((\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\big)\,.

Since
\cal{O}\cal{O}^{-1}=I
and in a system of coordinates
\nabla_{{\alpha}}\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O}-\nabla_{{\beta}}\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O}=0
, we get

\displaystyle\tilde{F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}\big(\nabla_{{\alpha}}A_{{\beta}}-\nabla_{{\beta}}A_{{\alpha}}+[A_{{\alpha}},A_{{\beta}}]\big)\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O})A_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}A_{{\beta}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O})A_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-\big(\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle-\big((\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle+\big((\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\big)\,.

On the other hand, since

\displaystyle\cal{O}\cal{O}^{-1}=I\,,

we have

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1})=0\,,

and thus,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}=-\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle\tilde{F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\cal{O})A_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}-\cal{O}A_{{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{{\beta}}\cal{O})A_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}A_{{\alpha}}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})(\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle-\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}+(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}A_{\beta}\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle+(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\beta}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}\,.

Now, let

\displaystyle\tilde{\Psi}=\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}\,,

we compute

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{\alpha}\tilde{\Psi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{\Psi}+[\tilde{A_{\alpha}},\tilde{\Psi}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{\Psi}+[\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1},\tilde{\Psi}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{\Psi}+\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}+\tilde{\Psi}\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\tilde{\Psi}+\tilde{\Psi}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{\alpha}(\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1})+\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}(\nabla_{\alpha}\Psi)\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}\Psi(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle+\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}\Psi A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}(\nabla_{\alpha}\Psi)\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}\Psi(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1})+\cal{O}[A_{\alpha},\Psi]\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle-\cal{O}\Psi\cal{O}^{-1}(\cal{O}\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}(\nabla_{\alpha}\Psi)\cal{O}^{-1}+\cal{O}[A_{\alpha},\Psi]\cal{O}^{-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\cal{O}(\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\alpha}\Psi)\cal{O}^{-1}\,.

Now, we state the following well-known lemma:

Lemma 4.2
.

If
(M,A,{\bf g})
is a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, then
(M,\tilde{A},{\bf g})
is also a solution which is what we call the gauge invariance of the equations.

Proof.

Let
F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}
be a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\big(<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\big)\;.

Since

\displaystyle<\tilde{F}_{\mu{\beta}},\tilde{F}_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}<\tilde{F}_{{\alpha}{\beta}},\tilde{F}^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<\cal{O}F_{\mu{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1},\cal{O}F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}>-\frac{1}{4}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}<\cal{O}F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1},\cal{O}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cal{O}^{-1}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;,

then,
\tilde{F}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}
is also a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{2}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\big(<\tilde{F}_{\mu{\beta}},\tilde{F}_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-\frac{1}{4}\cdot g_{\mu\nu}<\tilde{F}_{{\alpha}{\beta}},\tilde{F}^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\big)\;,

(4.3)

which will in turn enforce, by the symmetries of the Riemann tensor, that

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}T_{\mu\nu}(\tilde{F})=0\;,

and since we also have the Bianchi identities for
\tilde{F}
(given the expression of
\tilde{F}
in terms of the potential
\tilde{A}
), we also have

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{{\alpha}}\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu}+\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{\mu}\tilde{F}_{\nu{\alpha}}+\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{\nu}\tilde{F}_{{\alpha}\mu}\;,

(4.4)

which all together leads to

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{{\alpha}}\tilde{F}^{{\alpha}{\beta}}=\nabla_{\alpha}\tilde{F}^{{\alpha}{\beta}}+[\tilde{A}_{\alpha},\tilde{F}^{{\alpha}{\beta}}]=0\;.

(4.5)

This is consistent with the fact that

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{{\alpha}}\tilde{F}^{{\alpha}{\beta}}=\cal{O}(\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{{\alpha}}F^{{\alpha}{\beta}})\cal{O}^{-1}=0\;,

and that

\displaystyle\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{{\alpha}}\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu}+\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{\mu}\tilde{F}_{\nu{\alpha}}+\textbf{D}^{(\tilde{A})}_{\nu}\tilde{F}_{{\alpha}\mu}=\cal{O}(\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{{\alpha}}F_{\mu\nu}+\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\mu}F_{\nu{\alpha}}+\textbf{D}^{(A)}_{\nu}F_{{\alpha}\mu})\cal{O}^{-1}=0\;.

Consequently, for each solution
F
of the Einstein-Yang-Mills equation, we can make a gauge transformation

\displaystyle\tilde{A_{\alpha}}=\cal{O}A_{\alpha}\cal{O}^{-1}-(\nabla_{\alpha}\cal{O})\cal{O}^{-1}\,,

and define a new solution
\tilde{A}
, which is what we call the gauge invariance of the equations. Hence, a solution
A
to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system is only defined up to a class.

We know that a global existence result for the Yang-Mills fields for any arbitrary gauge on the Yang-Mills potential fails. In fact, given any global solution for the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, one can always perform a gauge transformation on the Yang-Mills potential so that the gauge transformed solution remains a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations but blows-up in finite time. Thus, fixing a gauge condition on the Yang-Mills fields is essential in order to obtain a global solution. We choose here to work in the Lorenz gauge, which impose on the solution to satisfy the following condition

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}A_{{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(4.6)

4.2.
The diffeomorphism invariance

Let
(\cal{M},A,{\bf g})
be a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system. Now, consider a diffeomorphism
\phi:\cal{M}\to\cal{M}^{\prime}
and define a metric
{\bf g}^{\prime}
on
\cal{M}^{\prime}
by
\phi^{*}{\bf g}^{\prime}={\bf g}
where
\phi^{*}
is the pull-back of
\phi
. In other words, at each point
p\in\cal{M}
, we define
{\bf g}^{\prime}
by

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(\phi_{*}X,\phi_{*}Y)(\phi(p))=g(X,Y)(p)\,,

where
\phi_{*}:T_{p}\cal{M}\to T_{p}\cal{M}^{\prime}
is the push-forward of
\phi
defined through

\displaystyle\phi_{*}X(f)(\phi(p))=X(\phi^{*}f)(p)\,,

for all smooth functions
f
on
\cal{M}^{\prime}
and for all
X\in T_{p}\cal{M}
, and where
\phi^{*}f(p):=f(\phi(p))
.

Then,
(\cal{M}^{\prime},A,{\bf g}^{\prime})
is also a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, which is what we call the diffeomorphism invariance of the equations. Hence, a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills system is only defined up to a class, where two solutions are the same if they are isometric. However, this gives us the freedom to choose a representative of this class.

Another way to look at the solution, is that one can eliminate the diffeomorphism invariance by fixing a system of coordinates. We choose to look at the manifold in harmonic coordinates, which
means that we are fixing our system of coordinates
\{x^{\mu}\}
such that:

\displaystyle\square_{{\bf g}}x^{\mu}:=\nabla^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}=0\,,

(4.7)

and such that
x^{0}=0
on
\Sigma
.

Since
x^{\mu}
are scalar functions on
\cal{M}
, and not tensors, we have,

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{{\alpha}}(x^{\mu}).

We evaluate

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}-\nabla_{\nabla^{{\alpha}}e_{\alpha}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}-{{\Gamma^{{\alpha}}}_{{\alpha}}}^{{\beta}}\nabla_{{\beta}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}-{{\Gamma^{{\alpha}}}_{{\alpha}}}^{{\beta}}\partial_{{\beta}}x^{\mu}\,,

where
\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}
are the Christoffel symbols. We have

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}=g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\beta}{\alpha}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mu}\,.

Computing the contraction, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}=\begin{cases}1,\quad\text{for}\quad{\alpha}=\mu\;,\\
0,\quad\text{for }\quad{\alpha}\neq\mu\;.\end{cases}

Hence,

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}=\partial^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}x^{\mu}=\partial^{\mu}1=0\,.

Thus,

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,{\beta}}\partial_{{\beta}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}\partial_{\mu}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}\,.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}x^{\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

is equivalent to

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{{\alpha}\nu}\Gamma_{\nu{\alpha}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\mu}=0\,.

(4.8)

Now, for any arbitrary tensor
\Psi
, we have

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Psi)-\nabla_{\nabla^{{\alpha}}e_{{\alpha}}}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Psi)-\nabla_{\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}e_{\mu}}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Psi)-\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}\nabla_{\mu}\Psi\,.

Consequently, either in harmonic coordinates or in a geodesic frame (i.e. a frame where the Christoffel symbols vanish), we can write

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Psi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Psi)\,.

(4.9)

Lemma 4.3
.

In either wave coordinates or in a geodesic frame, the Lorenz gauge can be written as

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}A_{{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(4.10)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}A_{{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}A_{{\alpha}}-A(\nabla^{{\alpha}}e_{{\alpha}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial^{{\alpha}}A_{{\alpha}}-\Gamma_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}^{{\alpha}\,\,\,\mu}A(e_{\mu})\,.

Thus, the result follows.

5.
Looking at the metric as a perturbation of the Minkowski space-time

Now that we have fixed the coordinates to be the wave coordinates, let
m
be Minkowski metric in these wave coordinates
\{x^{0},...,x^{n}\}
, i.e.
m
is the metric prescribed by:

\displaystyle m_{00}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle-1\;,\qquad m_{ii}:=1,\quad\text{if}\quad i=1,...,n\,,

\displaystyle\quad\text{and}\quad m_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle 0\;,\quad\text{if}\quad\mu\neq\nu\quad\text{for}\quad\mu,\nu\in\{0,1,...,n\}\,.

Definition 5.1
.

We define
h
as the 2-tensor given by:

\displaystyle h_{\mu\nu}:=g_{\mu\nu}-m_{\mu\nu}\;.

(5.1)

Let
m^{\mu\nu}
be the inverse of
m_{\mu\nu}
. We define

\displaystyle h^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle m^{\mu\mu^{\prime}}m^{\nu\nu^{\prime}}h_{\mu^{\prime}\nu^{\prime}}\;,

(5.2)

\displaystyle H^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle g^{\mu\nu}-m^{\mu\nu}\;.

(5.3)

Definition 5.2
.

Let
K
be a tensor that is either
A
or
h
or
H
, or
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A
,
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h
or
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}H
.

Let
P_{n}(K)
be tensors that are Polynomials of degree
n
, and
Q_{1}(K)
a tensor that is a Polynomial of degree
1
such that
Q_{1}(0)=0
and
Q_{1}\neq 0
, of which the coefficients are components in wave coordinates of the metric
m
and of the inverse metric
\textbf{m}^{-1}
, and of which the variables are components in wave coordinates of the covariant tensor
K
, leaving some indices free, so that the following product gives a tensor that we define as,

\displaystyle O_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{k}}(K)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle Q_{1}(K)\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}(K)\Big)\;.

For a family of tensors
K^{(1)},\ldots,K^{(m)}
, where each tensor
K^{(l)}
is again either
A
or
h
or
H
, or
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A
,
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h
or
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}H
, we define

\displaystyle O_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{k}}(K^{(1)}\cdot\ldots\cdot K^{(m)})

\displaystyle:=
