\displaystyle\square_{{\bf g}}A^{t}-R_{\mu}^{\,\,\,\,t}A^{\mu}+[\Lambda,A^{t}]+2[A_{{\alpha}},\nabla^{\alpha}A^{t}]-[A_{\alpha},\nabla^{t}A^{{\alpha}}]+[A_{\alpha},[A^{{\alpha}},A^{t}]]\,.

Since
(g_{\Sigma})_{ti}=0
for all
i\neq t
, we have
(g_{\Sigma})^{ti}=0
for all
i\neq t
. Thus,

\displaystyle g^{t\mu}\nabla_{t}\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{t\mu}\big(\square_{{\bf g}}A_{t}-R_{\mu t}A^{\mu}+[\Lambda,A_{t}]+2[A_{{\alpha}},\nabla^{\alpha}A_{t}]-[A_{\alpha},\nabla_{t}A^{{\alpha}}]+[A_{\alpha},[A^{{\alpha}},A_{t}]]\big)\,,

implies on
\Sigma
, that

\displaystyle g^{tt}\partial_{t}\Lambda

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g^{tt}\big(\square_{{\bf g}}A_{t}-R_{\mu t}A^{\mu}+[\Lambda,A_{t}]+2[A_{{\alpha}},\nabla^{\alpha}A_{t}]-[A_{\alpha},\nabla_{t}A^{{\alpha}}]+[A_{\alpha},[A^{{\alpha}},A_{t}]]\big)\,.

Since by construction of the initial data for the Yang-Mills potential
A_{\alpha}
, we have
\Lambda_{\Sigma}=0
, and since
(g_{\Sigma})^{tt}=-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\neq 0
, we obtain that on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\Lambda_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\square_{{\bf g}}A_{t}-R_{\mu t}A^{\mu}+[\Lambda,A_{t}]+2[A_{{\alpha}},\nabla^{\alpha}A_{t}]-[A_{\alpha},\nabla_{t}A^{{\alpha}}]+[A_{\alpha},[A^{{\alpha}},A_{t}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\square_{{\bf g}}A_{t}-R_{\mu t}A^{\mu}+2[A_{{\alpha}},\nabla^{\alpha}A_{t}]-[A_{\alpha},\nabla_{t}A^{{\alpha}}]+[A_{\alpha},[A^{{\alpha}},A_{t}]]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

(since the initial data for
A
was chosen such that it solves the Yang-Mills constraints (
3.12
),

which here reads as the equation (
8.3
) on
\Sigma
).

In summary, from (
8.3
) and Lemma
8.2
, we have

\displaystyle\square_{{\bf g}}\Lambda=[\nabla_{{\beta}}\Lambda,A^{{\beta}}]\,,

(8.18)

with
\Lambda_{\Sigma}=0
and
\partial_{t}\Lambda_{\Sigma}=0
. This is a wave equation in
\Lambda
with identically zero initial conditions, thus the unique global solution is
\Lambda=0
for all
t
.

8.4.
The propagation of the wave coordinates condition

We want to show that the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in the wave coordinate condition is consistent with the wave coordinates condition being imposed for all time, i.e. that the Einstein-Yang-Mills system in wave coordinates preserve the wave coordinates for all time
t
, if the initial data for the Einstein-Yang-Mills system satisfies some wave coordinate constraint.

Definition 8.1
.

Let

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}:=g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}\,.

(8.19)

Remark 8.2
.

Note that
\Gamma^{\lambda}
is not a tensor. However, the difference between two Christoffel symbols coming out from two different metrics, is a tensor. Thus, we view the wave coordinate condition as the difference of the two traces

\displaystyle 0=g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}-g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\hat{\Gamma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}(m)\,,

(8.20)

where
\hat{\Gamma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}(m)
are the Christoffel symbols of the metric
m
defined to be the Minkowski metric in wave coordinates. Hence, the wave coordinate condition can be viewed as a tensorial geometric quantity equal to zero, which in wave coordinates can be written as

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}=0\,,

(8.21)

since
\hat{\Gamma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}(m)=0
in wave coordinates.

Hence, when we write
\Gamma^{\lambda}=0
, this means that we already fixed the system of coordinates to be the wave coordinates, however we view it as the tensor given in wave coordinates by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}=g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}-g^{{\alpha}{\beta}}\hat{\Gamma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}^{\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\lambda}(m)

(8.22)

and we differentiate it using the Levi-Civita covariant derivative associated to the metric
g
.

In turns out, based on the proof of Lemma 3.1 in
[
38
]
, that for a solution of the system of non-linear wave equation on the metric, namely (
7.13
), the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations,
2.14
, read

\displaystyle R_{{\alpha}{\beta}}-2<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>-\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot g_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cdot<F_{\sigma\lambda},F^{\sigma\lambda}>=\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}})+\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\;,

where
N^{\sigma}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)
is a non-linearity depending on
g
and
\partial g
. In other words, in (
7.13
), the terms on the right hand side of (
8.4
) are the ones we added using the wave coordinates condition, where
\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
is identically zero (and therefore, all derivatives up to any order of
\Gamma_{{\beta}}
are null).

Now, we shall use this equation, (
8.4
), to show that
\Gamma
satisfies a non-linear wave equation.

Lemma 8.6
.

For a solution
g=m+h
of the non-linear wave equation given in (
7.13
), we have that the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations
2.14
imply in wave coordinates the following equation on
\Gamma_{{\beta}}
(defined in Definition
8.1
, and considering Remark
8.2
and in particular
8.22
),

\displaystyle\square_{\bf g}\Gamma_{{\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-2\big(\nabla^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big)N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)+\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big)N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)

(8.24)

\displaystyle-R_{\mu{\beta}}\Gamma^{\mu}-2\Gamma_{\sigma}\big(\nabla^{\alpha}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)+\Gamma_{\sigma}\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle-4\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>+\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;,

where
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita covariant derivative associate to the metric
{\bf g}
.

Proof.

On one hand, since
\nabla
is the Levi-Civita covariant derivative, we have the Bianchi identities for the Riemann tensor (see (
2.10
)), that

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}R_{{\alpha}{\beta}}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{{\beta}}R=0\;.

(8.25)

On the other hand, based on (
8.4
) and by differentiating, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}R_{{\alpha}{\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}})+\nabla^{\alpha}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{\alpha}\big(2<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>+\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot g_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\cdot<F_{\sigma\lambda},F^{\sigma\lambda}>\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}})+\nabla^{\alpha}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle+2\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>+\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot\nabla_{\beta}\cdot<F_{\sigma\lambda},F^{\sigma\lambda}>\;.

We compute
R
from (
8.4
), and we get

\displaystyle R=R_{{\alpha}}^{\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}+\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}})+\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)+2<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F_{{\alpha}{\beta}}>+\frac{(1+n)}{(1-n)}\cdot<F_{\sigma\lambda},F^{\sigma\lambda}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}+\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)+\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}\cdot<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;,

and therefore

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\beta}}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla_{{\beta}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)+\frac{(3-n)}{(1-n)}\cdot\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;.

Thus, injecting in the Bianchi identity, we obtain

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla^{{\alpha}}R_{{\alpha}{\beta}}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{{\beta}}R

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}})+\nabla^{\alpha}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}+\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle+2\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>+\frac{1}{(1-n)}\cdot\nabla_{\beta}\cdot<F_{\sigma\lambda},F^{\sigma\lambda}>-\frac{(3-n)}{2\cdot(1-n)}\cdot\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}})+\nabla^{\alpha}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}+\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle+2\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;.

Consequently, we have that
\Gamma_{\lambda}
satisfies the following equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2}\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{{\alpha}}+\nabla^{\alpha}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)-\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}+\nabla_{{\beta}}\big(\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)\big)

\displaystyle+2\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\square_{\bf g}\Gamma_{{\beta}}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}-\frac{1}{2}\nabla_{{\beta}}\nabla_{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{{\alpha}}\big)+\big(\nabla^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big)N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)+\Gamma_{\sigma}\big(\nabla^{\alpha}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big)N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)-\frac{1}{2}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle+2\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>-\frac{1}{2}\cdot\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;.

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\square_{\bf g}\Gamma_{{\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-R_{\,\,\,\mu{\alpha}{\beta}}^{{\alpha}}\Gamma^{\mu}-2\big(\nabla^{\alpha}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big)N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)-2\Gamma_{\sigma}\big(\nabla^{\alpha}N^{\sigma}_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(g,\partial g)\big)

(8.26)

\displaystyle+\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}\Gamma_{\sigma}\big)N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)+\Gamma_{\sigma}\big(\nabla_{{\beta}}N^{\sigma\,\,\,\,{\alpha}}_{\,\,\,{\alpha}}(g,\partial g)\big)

\displaystyle-4\nabla^{\alpha}<F_{{\alpha}\sigma},F_{{\beta}}^{\;\;\sigma}>+\nabla_{{\beta}}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;.

which gives the stated result.

Thus, the source terms for
\square_{\bf g}\Gamma_{{\beta}}
depend only on
{\bf g}
and
\Gamma_{{\beta}}
and their first derivatives only, as well as on
F
and the first derivatives of
F
. Consequently,
\Gamma_{{\beta}}
satisfies a non-linear wave equation. The initial data set for both
A
and
{\bf g}
on
\Sigma
was constructed in Subsections
8.1
and
8.2
, so that
\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
on
\Sigma
. Now, we would like to see if the wave coordinates condition propagates in time, so as to have
\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
on
\Sigma_{t}
for all time
t
.

In fact, since we already have
\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
on
\Sigma
, if in addition, we would have
\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
on
\Sigma
, then, we would have a non-linear wave equation for
\Gamma_{{\beta}}
(that we exhibited in Lemma
8.6
) with identically null initial data, and thus this would prove that
\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
is identically zero in the time evolution (and therefore, all derivatives up to any order of
\Gamma_{{\beta}}
are null) and, consequently,
\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
for
t\geq 0
. Hence, the condition
\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
if true on the initial slice
\Sigma
, it would guarantee that the wave coordinate gauge propagates in time
t
.

However, we did already construct the initial data
(g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
in wave coordinates, i.e. such that
\Gamma^{\lambda}=0
on
\Sigma
. Yet, we want to see if the whole initial data set
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
that we already constructed from our original initial data set solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations, namely
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
, would give this additional wave coordinate condition constraint, namely
\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{{\beta}}=0
on
\Sigma
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For a solution of the non-linear wave equations on the metric given in (
7.13
), with initial data solving the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations (
3.10
) and (
3.11
), and constructed as in Subsections
8.1
and
8.2
, we have in wave coordinates on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

and

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(8.28)

Proof.

We first recall that the Einstein-Yang-Mills system, (
2.15
), implies the following equation

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-g_{\mu\nu}\cdot\frac{1}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,.

Thanks to (
8.4
), we have for a solution of (
7.13
), that in wave coordinates,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu})+\Gamma_{\sigma}N^{\sigma}_{\mu\nu}(g,\partial g)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-g_{\mu\nu}\cdot\frac{1}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>,

Since on
\Sigma
, we constructed our initial data
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
in way such that
\Gamma_{\sigma}=0
, then the initial data constructed in Subsection
8.2
, gives that we have on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-g_{\mu\nu}\cdot\frac{1}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>\;.

Since we choose the initial data for our system in (
7.13
) such that it solves the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, namely by choosing the initial data as a solution to the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations given in Lemma
3.4
– in other words, we have the initial data set that we constructed in Subsections
8.1
and
8.2
, taken solutions to the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints derived in Subsection
3.2

–, therefore, we have on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle R_{\mu\nu}-2<F_{\mu{\beta}},F_{\nu}^{\;\;{\beta}}>-g_{\mu\nu}\cdot\frac{1}{(1-n)}<F_{{\alpha}{\beta}},F^{{\alpha}{\beta}}>=0

and thus, by injecting, we have on
\Sigma

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\mu}\Gamma_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}\Gamma_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

Hence, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\hat{t}}\Gamma_{\hat{t}}+\nabla_{\hat{t}}\Gamma_{\hat{t}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

Thus, solutions to the non-linear wave equation on the metric, given in (
7.13
), with initial data solving the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations and the wave coordinates condition, give that

\displaystyle\nabla_{\hat{t}}\Gamma_{\hat{t}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

Yet, on
\Sigma
, we have
\hat{t}=\frac{1}{N}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}
, thus,

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(8.31)

Also, we have for spatial indices
i
in wave coordinates, the following on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}(\nabla_{\hat{t}}\Gamma_{{\rm i}}+\nabla_{i}\Gamma_{\hat{t}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

However, since by construction
\Gamma_{\lambda}=0
on
\Sigma
, we have

\nabla_{i}\Gamma_{\hat{t}}=0\;,

and hence, we get on
\Sigma
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}\Gamma_{{\rm i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

8.5.
Construction of the initial data for the hyperbolic system given an initial data set that solves the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraints

Finally, we have proven, in this Section
8
, the following corollary.

Corollary 8.1
.

Assume that we are given an initial data set
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
that satisfies the Einstein-Yang-Mills constraint equations given in Lemma
3.4
, which are

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}+\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,i}\overline{k}_{j}^{\,\,\,j}-\overline{k}^{ij}\overline{k}_{ij}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4}{(n-1)}<\overline{E}_{i},\overline{E}^{i}>

\displaystyle+<\overline{D}_{i}\overline{A}_{j}-\overline{D}_{j}\overline{A}_{i}+[\overline{A}_{i},\overline{A}_{j}],\overline{D}^{i}\overline{A}^{j}-\overline{D}^{j}\overline{A}^{i}+[\overline{A}^{i},\overline{A}^{j}]>\,,

\displaystyle\overline{D}_{i}\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,j}-\overline{D}_{j}\overline{k}^{i}_{\,\,\,i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2<\overline{E}_{i},{\overline{F}_{j}}^{\,i}>\,,

\displaystyle\overline{D}^{i}\overline{E}_{i}+[\overline{A}^{i},\overline{E}_{i}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\,,

where
\overline{D}
is the Levi-Civita covariant derivative associated to the given Riemannian metric
\overline{g}
, and where the summation is carried only over spatial indices, and we raise indices with respect to
\overline{g}
.

Then, we can construct an initial data set
(\Sigma,A_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}A_{\Sigma},g_{\Sigma},\partial_{t}g_{\Sigma})
, as prescribed in

8.1
,
8.3
,
8.4
and
8.11
, for the coupled system of non-linear hyperbolic equations given in Lemma
7.4
, such that solving that system gives rise to a solution of the Einstein-Yang-Mills system that is in the Lorenz gauge and in wave coordinates for all time
t
.

9.
Set-up for the proof

9.1.
The rotations and the Lorentz boosts

At a point
p
in the space-time, let
x^{\mu}
be the wave coordinates, with
x^{0}=t
, and let

\displaystyle x_{{\beta}}=m_{\mu{\beta}}x^{\mu}\,,

where we raised and lowered indices with respect to the Minkowski metric
m
, defined in wave coordinates to be the Minkowski metric, i.e. in wave coordinates, we have
m_{tt}=-1
,
m_{ii}=1
,
m_{ti}=0
and
m_{ij}=\delta_{ij}
. Here
i
and
j
denote always spatial indices.
The Lorentz boosts and rotations are

\displaystyle Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}\,,

and they form a representation of the Lie algebra of the Lorentz group. Here, what we call Lorentz boosts are
L_{ti}
and the rotations are
L_{ij}
.
We also define the well-known space-time dilation vector field, or the scaling vector field, as

\displaystyle S=t\partial_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}x^{i}\partial_{i}\,.

The Lorentz boosts and rotations along with the scaling vector field
S
and the time and space translations
\partial_{t}
and
\partial_{x_{i}}
, form a representation of the Lie algebra of the Poincare group, which is the group of isometries of the Minkowski space-time, which we will call the Minkowski vector fields and will be denoted by
{\cal Z}
. Vector fields belonging to Minkowski vector fields will be denoted by
Z
, i.e.

\displaystyle Z\in{\cal Z}:=\big\{Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\;,\;S\;,\;\partial_{{\alpha}}\,\,|\,\,{\alpha}\;,\;{\beta}\in\{0,\ldots,n\}\big\}\quad\text{with}\quad x^{0}=t=-x_{0}.

(9.1)

Note that the family
{\cal Z}
has
\frac{(n^{2}+3n+4)}{2}
vector fields:
\frac{(n+1)\cdot n}{2}
vectors for the Lorentz boosts and rotations,
(n+1)
space-time translations and one scaling vector field. One can order them and assign to each vector an
\frac{(n^{2}+3n+4)}{2}
-dimensional integer index
(0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0)
. Hence, a collection of
k
vector fields from the family
{\cal Z}
, can be described by the set
I=(\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{k})
, where each
\iota_{i}
is an
\frac{(n^{2}+3n+4)}{2}
-dimensional integer, where
|I|=k=\sum_{i=1}^{k}|\iota_{i}|
, with
|\iota_{i}|=1
. Thus, we make the following definition:

Definition 9.1
.

We define

\displaystyle Z^{I}:=Z^{\iota_{1}}\ldots Z^{\iota_{k}}\quad\text{for}\quad I=(\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{k}),

(9.2)

where
\iota_{i}
is an
\frac{(n^{2}+3n+4)}{2}
-dimensional integer index, with
|\iota_{i}|=1
, and
Z^{\iota_{i}}
representing each a vector field from the family
{\cal Z}
.

For a tensor
T
, of arbitrary order, either a scalar or valued in the Lie algebra, we define the Lie derivative as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}T:={\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{\iota_{1}}}\ldots{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{\iota_{k}}}T\quad\text{for}\quad I=(\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{k}).

(9.3)

In addition, when we write
I=I_{1}+I_{2}
, it means that we divided the set
I
into two sets
I_{1}
and in
I_{2}
, while preserving the order of
I
in
I_{1}
and in
I_{2}
, i.e., if
I=(\iota_{1},\ldots,\iota_{k})
, then
I_{1}=(\iota_{i_{1}},\ldots,\iota_{i_{n}})
and
I_{2}=(\iota_{i_{n+1}},\ldots,\iota_{i_{k}})
, where
i_{1}<\ldots<i_{n}
and
i_{n+1}<\ldots<i_{k}
. By a sum
\sum_{I_{1}+I_{2}=I}
, we mean that we make the sum over all such partitions for a given
I
. With this convention, the Leibniz rule holds and reads for sufficiently smooth functions
f
and
g
,

\displaystyle Z^{I}(fg)=\sum_{I_{1}+I_{2}=I}(Z^{I_{1}}f)(Z^{I_{2}}g)\;.

(9.4)

9.2.
Weighted Klainerman-Sobolev inequality

We now state a weighted Klainerman-Sobolev inequality, see for example
[
7
]
or
[
31
]
for a proof. It is a weighted version of the standard Klainerman-Sobolev inequality. We define

\displaystyle q:=r-t\;,

(9.5)

which is a null coordinate for the Minkowski metric in wave coordinates. The weight is defined by the following

Definition 9.2
.

We define
w(q)
by

\displaystyle w(q):=\begin{cases}(1+|q|)^{1+2\gamma}\quad\text{when }\quad q>0\;,\\
1\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0\;,\end{cases}

(9.6)

for some
\gamma>0
. Note that we put the notational factor of
2
infront of
\gamma
since we are going to compute
\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}
, and this way, for that expression, this notational factor disappears (see (
10.8
)).

Then, we have globally the following pointwise estimate for any smooth scalar function
\phi
vanishing at spatial infinity, i.e.
\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})=0
,

\displaystyle|\phi(t,x)|\cdot(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}\cdot\big[(1+|q|)\cdot w(q)\big]^{1/2}\leq C\sum_{|I|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{I}\phi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\;,

where here the
L^{2}
norm is taken on
t=\text{constant}
slice.

9.3.
Definition of the norms

We recall that we defined
m
to be the Minkowski metric in wave coordinates
\{t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}\}
, such that

\displaystyle m_{00}=m(\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\frac{\partial}{\partial t})=-1\;,

and for the spatial coordinates
\{\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}},\ldots,\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{n}}\}
tangent to
\Sigma_{t}
prescribed by
t=constant
hypersurfaces, we have

\displaystyle m_{ij}=m(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}})=\delta_{ij}\,,

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker symbol, and

\displaystyle m_{i0}=m(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\frac{\partial}{\partial t})=0\,.

Denoting
t=x^{0}=-x_{0}
, we define for all
\mu,\nu\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, the following euclidian metric in wave coordinates

\displaystyle E_{\mu\nu}=E(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}})=m(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}},\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}})+2m(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\mu}},\frac{\partial}{\partial t})\cdot m(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\nu}},\frac{\partial}{\partial t})\,.

Then, we define for a scalar tensor
Q_{{\alpha}}
,

\displaystyle|Q|_{\text{scal}}^{2}

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle E^{\mu\nu}Q_{\mu}\cdot Q_{\nu}

(9.8)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E_{\mu\nu}Q^{\mu}\cdot Q^{\nu}\,,

where we took here the scalar product, and where one lowers and highers indices with respect to the metric
E
and where
E^{{\alpha}{\beta}}
is the inverse matrix of
E_{{\alpha}{\beta}}
.

Similarly, for a tensor
K_{\alpha}
valued in the Lie algebra associated to the Lie group
G
, we define

\displaystyle|K|^{2}_{\cal G}:=E^{\mu\nu}<K_{\mu},K_{\nu}>\,,

(9.9)

where here
<\,,\,>
is the Ad-invariant norm on the Lie algebra.

Similarly, we define the norms for tensors of arbitrarily order by taking a full contraction with respect to the euclidian metric
E
of the scalar product of a scalar tensor, or of the scalar product on the Lie algebra of a
{\cal G}
-valued tensor.

To lighten the notation, we will use the same notation for both the scalar product for scalar components or for
{\cal G}
-valued components. Also, we will drop the indices
\mid.\mid_{\text{scal}}
and
\mid.\mid_{\cal G}
and use
\mid.\mid
for norms on tensors.

Using this notation, and viewing the gradient of a sufficiently smooth scalar function
f
as the tensor
\partial_{a}f
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial f|^{2}:=E^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}f\ \cdot\partial_{\nu}f\;,

(9.10)

which is a definition that generalises for taking instead of
f
, a tensor of arbitrarily order, either a scalar tensor or valued in the Lie algebra
\cal G
, by replacing the partial derivatives with a covariant derivative with respect to the Minkowski metric. We will be more precise in the definitions in what follows.

Definition 9.3
.

Now, defining the connection
\nabla^{(\bf{m})}
to be the flat connection in the wave coordinates such that its Christoffel symbols are vanishing in wave coordinates, i.e. such that for all
\mu,\nu\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
,

\displaystyle{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{e_{\mu}}e_{\nu}:=0\;,

(9.11)

where
e_{\mu}=\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\mu}}
and where
\{x^{0},x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}\}
are the wave coordinates.

We then define for a scalar tensor
Q_{{\alpha}}

\displaystyle|\partial Q|^{2}:=E^{{\alpha}{\beta}}E^{\mu\nu}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}Q_{\alpha}\ \cdot{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\nu}Q_{\beta},

(9.12)

and similarly for scalar tensors of arbitrarily order. We define for a tensor
K_{\alpha}
valued in the Lie algebra

\displaystyle|\partial K|^{2}:=E^{{\alpha}{\beta}}E^{\mu\nu}<{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}K_{{\alpha}},{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\nu}K_{{\beta}}>\,,

(9.13)

and similarly for scalar tensors of arbitrary order. Note that by contracting in wave coordinates, we get

\displaystyle|\partial K|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{t}K|^{2}+|{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{x^{1}}K|^{2}+\ldots+|{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{x^{n}}K|^{2}

(9.14)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{{\alpha},\;{\beta}\in\{t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}\}}|\partial_{{\alpha}}K_{\beta}|^{2}\,,

since in wave coordinates, the Minkowski covariant derivative of the contraction of a tensor expressed in wave coordinates, is in fact a partial derivative. We shall also write

\displaystyle|{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}K|:=|\partial K|\;.

(9.15)

Lemma 9.1
.

At a point
p
of the space-time, let
x^{\mu}
be the wave coordinate system. For a sufficiently smooth function
f
and for a norm
\mid.\mid
, we define for all
I
and
Z^{I}
as previously defined, the following norm in the wave coordinates system
\{t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}\}
,

\displaystyle|Z^{I}\partial f|:=\sqrt{|Z^{I}\partial_{t}f|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}|Z^{I}\partial_{i}f|^{2}}\;.

(9.16)

Then, we have,

\displaystyle|Z^{I}\partial f|\leq C(|I|)\cdot\sum_{|J|\leq|I|}|\partial(Z^{J}f)|\;,

(9.17)

where
C(|I|)
is a constant that depends only on
|I|
.

Proof.

Recall that

\displaystyle x_{{\beta}}=m_{\mu{\beta}}x^{\mu}\;,

and

\displaystyle Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}=x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}\;.

Computing for a sufficiently smooth function
f
,

\displaystyle[\partial_{\mu},Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}]f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f)-Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(\partial_{\mu}f)=\partial_{\mu}(x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f)-(x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}})\partial_{\mu}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(x_{{\beta}})\partial_{{\alpha}}f+x_{{\beta}}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-\partial_{\mu}(x_{{\alpha}})\partial_{{\beta}}f-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{{\beta}}f-x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}f+x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}\partial_{\mu}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(x_{{\beta}})\partial_{{\alpha}}f-\partial_{\mu}(x_{{\alpha}})\partial_{{\beta}}f\;.

However, we have,

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(x_{{\beta}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(m_{\sigma{\beta}}x^{\sigma})=\partial_{\mu}(m_{\sigma{\beta}})x^{\sigma}+m_{\sigma{\beta}}\partial_{\mu}(x^{\sigma})=m_{\sigma{\beta}}\delta_{\mu}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m_{\mu{\beta}}\;.

Hence,

\displaystyle[\partial_{\mu},Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}]f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f)-Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}(\partial_{\mu}f)=m_{\mu{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-m_{\mu{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f\;.

Thus,

\displaystyle[\partial_{\mu},Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}]=\begin{cases}m_{\mu{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-m_{\mu{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f=0\quad\text{ if}\quad({\alpha}={\beta}),\quad\text{ or if}\quad(\mu\neq{\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad\mu\neq{\beta}),\\
m_{\mu{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-m_{\mu{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f=m_{{\beta}{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}\quad\text{if}\quad\mu={\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad{\alpha}\neq{\beta},\\
m_{\mu{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-m_{\mu{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f=-m_{{\alpha}{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}\quad\text{if}\quad\mu={\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad{\alpha}\neq{\beta}\;.\\
\end{cases}

(9.18)

Thus, we conclude that for a sufficiently smooth function
f
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\partial_{\mu}f=\begin{cases}\partial_{\mu}(Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f)\quad\text{ if}\quad({\alpha}={\beta})\quad\text{ or}\quad(\mu\neq{\alpha}\quad\text{ and}\quad\mu\neq{\beta}),\\
\partial_{\mu}(Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f)-m_{{\beta}{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f\quad\text{if}\quad\mu={\beta}\quad\text{and}\quad{\alpha}\neq{\beta},\\
\partial_{\mu}(Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f)+m_{{\alpha}{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f\quad\text{if}\quad\mu={\alpha}\quad\text{and}\quad{\alpha}\neq{\beta}\;.\\
\end{cases}

(9.19)

Thus,

\displaystyle\mid Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}\partial_{\mu}f\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid\partial_{\mu}(Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f)\mid+\mid\partial_{{\alpha}}f\mid+\mid\partial_{{\beta}}f\mid\,.

(9.20)

Moreover, considering a commutation with the scaling vector field

\displaystyle S=t\partial_{t}+\sum_{i=1}^{3}x^{i}\partial_{i}=\sum_{{\alpha}=0}^{3}x^{\alpha}\partial_{{\alpha}}\,,

we get

\displaystyle[\partial_{\mu},S]f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(Sf)-S(\partial_{\mu}f)=\partial_{\mu}(x^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f)-x^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}(\partial_{\mu}f)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(x^{{\alpha}})\partial_{{\alpha}}f+x^{{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-x^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}\partial_{\mu}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}(x^{{\alpha}})\partial_{{\alpha}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta_{\mu}^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}f\,.

Consequently,

\displaystyle|S\partial_{\mu}f|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\partial_{\mu}(Sf)|+|\partial_{\mu}f|.

(9.21)

Now, in wave coordinates,
|\partial f|
is defined to be

\displaystyle|\partial f|:=\sqrt{|\partial_{t}f|^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\partial_{i}f|^{2}}\,.

(9.22)

Thus, in wave coordinates
x^{\mu}
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial f|\geq|\partial_{\mu}f|\,,

and as a result, we have shown that for all
Z\in{\cal Z}
, we have in wave coordinates, the following estimate

\displaystyle|Z\partial_{\mu}f|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\partial(Zf)|+|\partial f|.

(9.23)

Let
Z^{\iota_{j}}
,
j\in\{1,2,...,k\}
be a family of vector fields from the family
{\cal Z}
. By induction, we get that there exists a constant
C_{1}(k)
, depending on
k
, such that

\displaystyle|\prod_{j=1}^{k}Z^{\iota_{j}}\partial_{\mu}f|\leq C_{1}(k)\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{k}|\partial(\prod_{j=0}^{i}Z^{\iota_{j}}f)|,

which leads to

\displaystyle|Z^{I}\partial_{\mu}f|\leq C_{2}(|I|)\cdot\sum_{|J|\leq|I|}|\partial(Z^{J}f)|.

(9.24)

9.4.
The energy norm

We recall that we are given an initial data set which we write as
(\Sigma,\overline{A},\overline{E},\overline{g},\overline{k})
, and that
\Sigma
is diffeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, and therefore there exists a global system of coordinates
(x^{1},...,x^{n})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
for
\Sigma
, and we define

\displaystyle r:=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+...+(x^{n})^{2}}\;.

(9.25)

We assume that the initial data set is smooth and asymptotically flat. Now, this initial data set looks differently depending on the space-dimension
n
. Let us explain: if we define
M(n)
, to be the mass, defined by

\displaystyle M(n):=\begin{cases}M>0\quad\text{for }\quad n=3\;,\\
0\quad\text{for }\quad n\geq 4\;,\end{cases}

(9.26)

and if we define a smooth function
\chi
, given by

\displaystyle\chi(r):=\begin{cases}1\quad\text{for }\quad r\geq\frac{3}{4}\;,\\
0\quad\text{for }\quad r\leq\frac{1}{2}\;,\end{cases}

(9.27)

and if we let
\delta_{ij}
be the Kronecker symbol, and if we define
\overline{h}^{1}_{ij}
in this system of coordinates
x^{i}
, by

\displaystyle\overline{h}^{1}_{ij}:=\overline{g}_{ij}-(1+\chi(r)\cdot\frac{M(n)}{r})\delta_{ij}\;,

(9.28)

then, the initial data can be written as

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{ij}=\overline{h}^{1}_{ij}+\delta_{ij}+\chi(r)\cdot\frac{M(n)}{r}\delta_{ij}\;.

(9.29)

We can then define

\displaystyle\overline{h}_{ij}=\overline{h}^{1}_{ij}++\chi(r)\cdot\frac{M(n)}{r}\delta_{ij}\;.

(9.30)

and this way, we can write

\displaystyle\overline{g}_{ij}=\overline{h}_{ij}++\delta_{ij}\;.

(9.31)

However, in the case
n\geq 4
, the mass
M(n)=0
, and thus, on the initial slice
\Sigma
, we have
\overline{h}=\overline{h}^{1}
. Hence, in higher dimensions, we look for a solution in the following form in wave coordinates,

\displaystyle g_{\mu\nu}=h_{\mu\nu}+m_{\mu\nu}\;,

(9.32)

where
h
is the propagation of
\overline{h}^{1}=\overline{h}
(n higher dimension).
We want to define the energy as a quantity in a form that could dominate the right-hand side of the weighted Klainerman-Sobolev inequality for the functions
\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\mu\nu}
and
\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}
,
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
, instead of
\phi
, and for higher order
N
. We note that such a definition for the energy would not give a finite energy for
\chi(r)\cdot\frac{2\cdot M(n)}{r}
, that is the part that carries the mass
M(n)
. Thus, in
n=3
, we need to write instead
h=h^{1}+h^{0}
, where
h^{0}
represents the propagation of the mass
M
(we shall explain this more in the third paper that follows).

Yet, keeping the discussion above in mind and the fact that we aim to study the case of
n=3
in a paper that follows, we shall often write the equations on
h^{1}
(instead of
h
), with

\displaystyle h^{1}=h-h^{0}\;,

(9.33)

where
h^{0}
is vanishing in higher dimensions, as in this paper.

In fact, we define the higher order energy norm as the following
L^{2}
norms on
A
and
h^{1}
, using the scalar products either on the Lie algebra
\cal G
or the usual scalar product, and we set

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t):=\sum_{|I|\leq N}\big(\|w^{1/2}\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{2}}+\|w^{1/2}\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{2}}\big)\,,

(9.34)

where the integration is taken with respect to the Lebesgue measure
dx_{1}\ldots dx_{n}
.

Here, we have in wave coordinates
(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle\mid\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)\mid^{2}:=\mid\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{t}\mid^{2}+\mid\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{1}\mid^{2}+\ldots+\mid\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{n}\mid^{2}\,,

where for
\mu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle\mid\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\mid^{2}:=\mid\partial_{t}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\mid^{2}+\mid\partial_{x^{1}}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\mid^{2}+\ldots+\mid\partial_{x^{n}}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\mid^{2}\,,

and similarly for the metric
h_{\mu\nu}^{1}
using the absolute value and a summation over all indices
\mu,\nu
in wave coordinates.

However, since for
n\geq 4
, we have
h=h^{1}
, and in particular for the case
n=5
that we consider here, we therefore write

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t):=\sum_{|I|\leq N}\big(\|w^{1/2}\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{2}}+\|w^{1/2}\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h(t,\cdot))\|_{L^{2}}\big)\,.

(9.37)

To sum up: we shall nevertheless often use in many equations, in this paper, the tensor
h^{1}
which coincides with
h
in the case of higher dimensions, since our goal is to continue the work in the third paper that follows where the part that carries the mass
M
is non-vanishing. Thus, we shall often refer to the energy as defined in (
9.34
).

9.5.
The bootstrap argument

It is a continuity argument. We start with a local solution defined on a maximum time interval
[0,T_{\text{loc}})
and that is well-posed in the energy norm
{\cal E}_{N}(t)
for some
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
. This means that the time dependance of the energy
{\cal E}_{N}(t)
is continuous: in other words, the map
[0,T_{\text{loc}})\to{\mathbb{R}}
, which assigns
t\to{\cal E}_{N}(t)
is continuous in the standard sense. Furthermore, by maximality of
T_{\text{loc}}
and the well-posedness of the solution, the time interval for the local solution must be excluding
T_{\text{loc}}
, otherwise the energy will be finite at
t=T_{\text{loc}}
and this means that we could extend the local solution again beyond the time
t=T_{\text{loc}}
by repeating the argument for establishing a local solution starting at time
t=T_{\text{loc}}
. In other words, the maximal
T_{\text{loc}}
is characterised by

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T_{\text{loc}}}{\cal E}_{N}(t)=\infty\;.

We look at any time
T\in[0,T_{\text{loc}})
, such that for all
t
in the interval of time
[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\cdot\epsilon\cdot(1+t)^{\delta}\;,

(9.38)

where
E(N)
is a constant that depends on
N
, where
\epsilon\geq 0
is a constant to be chosen later small enough, and where
\delta\geq 0
is to be chosen later. In addition, we start with an initial data such that this estimate holds true for
t=0
, i.e.

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(0)\leq E(N)\cdot\epsilon\;,

(9.39)

and thus we know that such a
T
exists, since at least
T=0
satisfies the estimate.

We will then show that for
t\in[0,T]
, the same estimate holds true but with
\epsilon
replaced with
\frac{\epsilon}{2}
, i.e. we then prove that for all
t
in the time interval
[0,T]
,

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot(1+t)^{\delta}\;.

(9.40)

As a result, we would have shown that the set

\{T\;\;|\;\text{for all}\quad t\in[0,T]\,,\quad{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\cdot\epsilon\cdot(1+t)^{\delta}\}

is relatively open in
[0,T_{\text{loc}})
, non-empty since
0
belongs to the set, and we know that it is relatively closed in
[0,T_{\text{loc}})
since the map
t\to{\cal E}_{N}(t)
is continuous, and thus, the set is the whole interval
[0,T_{\text{loc}})
.

Consequently, we would have shown that for all
t\in[0,T_{\text{loc}})
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot(1+t)^{\delta}\;.

As a result, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to T_{\text{loc}}}{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{2}\cdot(1+T_{\text{loc}})^{\delta}<\infty\;.

By continuity of the energy, this means that
{\cal E}_{N}(T_{\text{loc}})
is finite and we can then repeat the argument for establishing a local solution starting at time
t=T_{\text{loc}}
which would lead to a local solution defined beyond the time
t=T_{\text{loc}}
, which contradicts the maximality of
T_{\text{loc}}
.

To sum this up, we started by an a priori estimate (
9.38
), we improved the a priori estimate in (
9.40
), and we therefore showed using the local well-posedness of the solution that it is an actual estimate. Since the estimate (
9.38
) is therefore true, this provides the finiteness of the higher order energy
{\cal E}_{N}(t)
for all time
t
and therefore that the local solution for (
7.4
) is a in fact a global solution and furthermore, the improved estimate on the energy is true for all time
t
.

9.6.
The bootstrap assumption

As explained above, to run our bootstrap argument, we start by assuming that for all
k\leq c\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where
c
is to be determined later, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}(t)\leq E(k)\cdot\epsilon\cdot(1+t)^{\delta}\;.

(9.41)

In the case here, where
n\geq 5
, and also for the case that follows in the next paper for
n=4
, we choose in fact

\displaystyle\delta

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;,

(9.42)

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\;.

(9.43)

However, we carry out the calculations sometimes with
\delta\geq 0
, and always with
0<\epsilon\leq 1
, since in the case of
n=3
, in a paper that follows, we will use indeed
\delta>0
and we shall indeed fix
\epsilon
small, and we would like therefore to use some of the calculations carried out here without repeating them. Thus, our bootstrap assumption here is

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}(t)\leq E(k)\cdot\epsilon\;.

(9.44)

The choice, for next papers, of

\displaystyle 0<\epsilon\leq 1\;,

(9.45)

is so that any powers of
\epsilon
are in fact bounded by
\epsilon
. To lighten the notation, we also choose here

\displaystyle E(k)\leq 1\;,

(9.46)

so that any sum of powers of
E(k)
is in fact bounded by a constant multiplied by
E(k)
. In addition, we choose

\displaystyle E(k_{1})\leq E(k_{2})\;,

(9.47)

for all
k_{1}\leq k_{2}
, with
k_{1}\,,k_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
, given the fact that
{\cal E}(k_{1})\leq{\cal E}(k_{2})
. The reason we choose to put the constants
E(k)
, rather than want an
\epsilon
to be fixed, is to show in the estimates the dependance on the energy and mainly, on the number of Lie derivatives involved. In other words, these constants
E(k)
are not needed but are there to make clearer in the argument the number of Lie derivatives of fields for which we use the bootstrap assumption. Speaking of this, in turns out in fact, that we could close the argument for
{\cal E}_{N}
, as in (
9.38
) with
\delta=0
and
\epsilon=1
, by assuming (
9.44
) for all

\displaystyle k\leq\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1\;,

provided that
N\geq 2\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+2
(see Proposition
17.1
).

To sum up, our actual bootstrap assumption for this paper is that for
k\leq\lfloor\frac{N}{2}\rfloor+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1
, with
N\geq 2\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+2
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{k}(t)\leq E(k)\;,

(9.48)

where
E(k)\leq 1
and
E(k_{1})\leq E(k_{2})
for all
k_{1}\,,k_{2}\in{\mathbb{N}}
. And we are looking forward to upgrading the estimate (
9.48
). For this, we will have to exploit the special structure of the equations.

In Section
10
, we will show how an a priori estimate on the energy, (
9.41
), translates into decay estimates on the pointwise norm of the solution that is the metric and the Yang-Mills potential – this is derived using the weighted Klainerman-Sobolev inequality.

9.7.
The
O
notation

Definition 9.4
.

For a family of tensors Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I_{1}}}K^{(1)},\ldots,{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I_{m}}}K^{(m)}
, where each tensor
K^{(l)}
is again either
A
or
h
or
H
, or
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}A
,
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}h
or
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}H
, we define

\displaystyle O_{\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{k}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I_{1}}}K^{(1)}\cdot\ldots\cdot{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I_{m}}}K^{(m)})

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\prod_{l=1}^{m}\Big[\prod_{|J_{l}|\leq|I_{l}|}Q_{1}^{J_{l}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{(l)})\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}^{J_{l}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{(l)})\Big)\Big]\;.

where again
P_{n}^{J_{l}}(K^{l})
and
Q_{1}^{J_{l}}(K)
, are tensors that are Polynomials of degree
n
and
1
, respectively, with
Q^{J_{l}}_{1}(0)=0
and
Q^{J_{l}}_{1}\neq 0
, of which the coefficients are components in wave coordinates of the metric
m
and of the inverse metric
\textbf{m}^{-1}
, and of which the variables are components in wave coordinates of the covariant tensor
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{l}
, leaving some indices free, so that at the end the whole product

\prod_{l=1}^{m}\Big[\prod_{|J_{l}|\leq|I_{l}|}Q_{1}^{J_{l}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{(l)})\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}^{J_{l}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{(l)})\Big)\Big]

gives a tensor with free indices
\mu_{1}\ldots\mu_{k}
. To lighten the notation, we shall drop the indices and just write
O({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I_{1}}}K^{(1)}\cdot\ldots\cdot{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I_{m}}}K^{(m)})
.

Remark 9.1
.

Note that if we use a bootstrap assumption, (
9.41
), to bound

Q_{1}^{|I_{l}|}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{|I_{l}|}}K^{(l)})\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}^{|I_{l}|}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{|I_{l}|}}K^{(l)})\Big)\;,

the bound will then hold true for

\prod_{|J_{l}|\leq|I_{l}|}^{m}Q_{1}^{J_{l}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{(l)})\cdot\Big(\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}P_{n}^{J_{l}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J_{l}}}K^{(l)})\Big)\;.

10.
A priori decay estimates

The a priori estimates are decay estimates that are generated from the weighted Klainerman-Sobolev inequality combined with the bootstrap assumption (
9.41
) which is the fact that we look at a time
T\in[0,T_{\text{loc}})
, such that for all
t\in[0,T]
, we have

\displaystyle{\cal E}_{N}(t)\leq E(N)\cdot\epsilon\cdot(1+t)^{\delta}\;.

This will generate decay estimates which have nothing to do with the Einstein-Yang-Mills equations, but they come from the fact that we chose the energy to be in the form of what dominates the right hand side of the Klainerman-Sobolev inequality when applied to
\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}
and
\partial Z^{I}A
. In other words, this bootstrap assumption
9.41
, is an assumption on the bound of such an energy (an assumption that needs yet to be improved in order to turn it into a true estimate) translates into pointwise decay estimates through Klainerman-Sobolev inequality. The fact that these estimates are generated from the bootstrap assumption, and are not proven yet to be true estimates, is the reason why we call them “a priori decay estimates”.

Lemma 10.1
.

Under the bootstrap assumption (
9.41
), taken for
N=|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1
, if for all
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
, and for any functions
\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\nu}\;,\;\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, then we have

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0,\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0.\end{cases}

Proof.

In fact, the weighted Sobolev estimate gives that for all
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
, and for any functions
\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\nu}\,,\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}\in C^{\infty}_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, i.e. if they are smooth functions vanishing at spatial infinity,

\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\nu}(t,x)=\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,x)=0,

and for any arbitrary
(t,x)
,

\displaystyle|\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,x)|\cdot(1+t+|q|)\cdot\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}\leq C\sum_{|J|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{J}}A_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}},

and

\displaystyle|\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\nu}(t,x)|\cdot(1+t+|q|)\cdot\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}\leq C\sum_{|J|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}.

However, we have established that for a sufficiently smooth function
f
,

\displaystyle|w^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial Z^{I}f(t,\cdot)|\leq C_{1}(|J|)\sum_{|K|\leq|J|}|w^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}Z^{I}f(t,\cdot)|,

which leads to

\displaystyle|w^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial Z^{I}f(t,\cdot)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{2}(|J|)\sum_{|K|\leq|J|}|w^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}Z^{I}f(t,\cdot)|^{2}

\displaystyle\text{(using $ab\lesssim a^{2}+b^{2}$ )}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial Z^{I}f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{3}(|J|)\sum_{|K|\leq|J|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}Z^{I}f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

(using
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|,|J|)\cdot\sum_{|K|\leq|I|+|J|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}f(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}.

Hence, for all
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
, we have

\displaystyle\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|,|J|)\sum_{|K|\leq|J|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\;.

Using the fact that a commutation of two vector fields in
\cal Z
, i.e.
[Z^{\iota_{i}},Z^{\iota_{k}}]
, gives a combination of vector fields in
\cal Z
, and using the fact that we have already showed, that a commutation of a vector field in
\cal Z
and
\partial_{\mu}
gives a linear combination of vectors of the form
\partial_{\mu}
, we get that for all
\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,
Z^{K}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}
is a linear combination of elements of the form
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{L}}A_{\mu}
with
|L|\leq|K|+|I|
and
\mu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
. Hence, for any
\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{|L|\leq|K|+|I|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{K}}A(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\,,

and therefore,

\displaystyle\sum_{|K|\leq|J|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial Z^{K}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\lesssim\sum_{|K|\leq|I|+|J|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{K}}A(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\,.

Consequently, for any
\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|,|J|)\sum_{|K|\leq|I|+|J|}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{K}}A(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|,|J|)\cdot{\cal E}_{|I|+|J|}(t),

and

\displaystyle\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\nu}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|,|J|)\cdot{\cal E}_{|I|+|J|}(t).

Plugging these estimates to the right hand side of the weighted Sobolev inequalities, (
9.2
), gives that for all
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle|\partial_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\nu}(t,x)|\cdot(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}\cdot\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\sum_{|J|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}\|\big(w(q)\big)^{1/2}Z^{J}\partial Z^{I}A(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{|J|\leq\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}C(|I|,|J|)\cdot{\cal E}_{|I|+|J|}(t)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t),

and hence,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t,x)|\cdot(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}\cdot\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t),

and similarly,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(t,x)|\cdot(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}\cdot\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t).

Thus,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t,x))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot\frac{{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t)}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}},

(10.6)

and

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(t,x))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot\frac{{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t)}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}}.

(10.7)

By definition of the weight
w
(see Definition
9.2
), for some
0<\gamma<1
, we have

\displaystyle(w(q))^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\big[(1+|q|)^{1+2\gamma}\big]^{1/2},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
1\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0.\end{cases}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}+\gamma},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
1\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0.\end{cases}

Hence,

\displaystyle\big[(1+|q|)w(q)\big]^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0\;,\\
(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0\;.\end{cases}

(10.8)

Consequently,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot\frac{{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t)}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0\;,\\
C(|I|)\cdot\frac{{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t)}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0\;,\end{cases}

and

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot\frac{{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t)}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0\;,\\
C(|I|)\cdot\frac{{\cal E}_{|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1}(t)}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0\;.\end{cases}

Using the bootstrap assumption on the growth of the higher order energy, we get

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon(1+t)^{\delta}}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon(1+t)^{\delta}}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon(1+t+|q|)^{\delta}}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon(1+t+|q|)^{\delta}}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0.\end{cases}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0,\end{cases}

and similarly,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}},\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0.\end{cases}

10.1.
The spatial asymptotic behaviour of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,x)
at
t=0

Lemma 10.2
.

We have for all vector
Z\in{\cal Z}
, and for all sufficiently smooth function
f
, the following estimate for
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|Zf|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+t+|x|)\cdot|\partial f|.

Proof.

As a reminder, in wave coordinates
x^{\mu}
, we have

\displaystyle Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}},

\displaystyle S

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle x^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}},

where

\displaystyle x_{{\beta}}=m_{\mu{\beta}}x^{\mu}.

Thus, we have,

\displaystyle|Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f-x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f|\leq|x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f|+|x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f|+|x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f|+|x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f|\;.

Hence, in wave coordinates, i.e. for
{\alpha},{\beta}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
, we have

\displaystyle|Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}}f|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|x_{{\beta}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f|+|x_{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\beta}}f|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(|t|+|x|\cdot|\partial f|,

where

\displaystyle|x|=\sqrt{(x^{1})^{2}+\ldots+(x^{n})^{2}}.

(10.11)

For the vector
S
, we have

\displaystyle|Sf|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|x^{{\alpha}}\partial_{{\alpha}}f|=|t\partial_{t}f+\sum_{i=0}^{n}x^{i}\partial_{i}f|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(|t|+|x|)\cdot|\partial f|.

Also, from the definition
|\partial f|
in wave coordinates, we get

\displaystyle|\partial_{x_{\alpha}}f|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\partial f|.

Consequently, for
Z\in\{Z_{{\alpha}{\beta}},S,\partial_{{\alpha}}\}
,
{\alpha},{\beta}\in\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
,

\displaystyle|Zf|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+|t|+|x|)\cdot|\partial f|\,.

(10.12)

Lemma 10.3
.

If the factor
\gamma
in the weight is such that
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-\frac{(n-1)}{2}\}
, then under the bootstrap assumption (
9.41
), taken for
k=|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1
, we have for all
|I|\geq 1
,

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,x)|+|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(0,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+r)^{1+\gamma-\delta}}\;,

and

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,x)|+|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(0,x)|\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

(10.14)

Also, we choose to take the initial data such that (
10.3
) is also true for
|I|=0
, which implies (
10.14
).

Remark 10.1
.

In addition, for such
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-\frac{(n-1)}{2}\}
, we also have

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)A(0,x)|+|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)h^{1}(0,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+r)^{1+\gamma-\delta}}\;,

and

\displaystyle\lim_{r=\to\infty}\big(|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(0,x)|+|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(0,x)|\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0\;.

Proof.

Since
q=r-t
, at
t=0
, we have
q=r\geq 0
. We have established that for
q\geq 0
,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t,x)|+|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}}\;.

Plugging in
t=0
, we get

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(0,x)|+|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(0,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+1+\gamma-\delta}}\;.

This means that for
\gamma>\delta-1-\frac{(n-1)}{2}=\delta-\frac{(n+1)}{2}
, and
\gamma>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r=|x|\to\infty}\big(|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(0,x)|+|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(0,x)|\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\lim_{|q|\to\infty}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|q|)^{\frac{(n+1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}=0\;.

In particular, this implies that for
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-\frac{(n+1)}{2}\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r=|x|\to\infty}\big(|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1})(0,x)|+|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(0,x)|\big)=0\;.

Now, we would like to estimate the asymptotics of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,x)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h(0,x)
.

For
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
, taking in Lemma
10.2
,
f={\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,x)
and then
f={\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h_{\mu\nu}(0,x)
, we obtain for all
I
, and for any vector
Z\in{\cal Z}
,

\displaystyle|\ Z{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,x)|+|\ Z{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\mu\nu}(0,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+|t|+|x|)\cdot(|\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,x)|+|\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(0,x)|)\;.

Since at
t=0
, we have
q=|x|
, we get for any
\mu,\nu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle|\ Z{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,x)|+|\ Z{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\mu\nu}(0,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+|q|)\cdot(|\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,x)|+|\partial{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(0,x)|)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle(1+|q|)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+1+\gamma-\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}\;.

Now, using the fact that a commutation of two vector fields in
\cal Z
is again a combination of vector fields in
\cal Z
, and using the fact that a commutation of a vector field in
\cal Z
and
\partial_{\mu}
gives a linear combination of vectors of the form
\partial_{\mu}
, we get by induction on
|I|
that for all
I
such that
|I|\geq 1
,

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,x)|+|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}_{\mu\nu}(0,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}\,.

In particular, this means that if
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-\frac{(n-1)}{2}\}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\big(|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,x)|+|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(0,x)|\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(10.21)

For
|I|=0
, we take the initial data such that

\displaystyle|A(0,x)|+\mid h^{1}(0,x)\mid

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|r|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}

which implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\big(|A(0,x)|+|h^{1}(0,x)|\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(10.22)

10.2.
Estimates on
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}
for
t>0
.

Now, we will use (
10.3
) in Lemma
10.3
to estimate the Lie derivatives in the direction of Minkowski vector fields of the Einstein-Yang-Mills fields
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}
, for
t>0
. This will be done by specific integration till we reach the hyperplane prescribed by
t=
and then use (
10.3
).

Lemma 10.4
.

Under the bootstrap assumption (
9.41
), taken for
k=|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1
, and with
\gamma>0
and with initial data such that

\displaystyle|A(0,x)|+|h^{1}(0,x)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{(1+r)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}\;,

then, we have for all
|I|
,

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,x)|+|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(t,x)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\begin{cases}c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}},\quad\text{when }\quad q>0\;,\\
C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon\cdot(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\,\quad\text{when }\quad q<0\;.\end{cases}

Proof.

Let
B
and
C
be tensors. For a scalar product
<B,C>
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}<B,C>

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle<\partial_{\mu}B,C>+<B,\partial_{\mu}C>

– which is the case for the definition for our norms –, we have (see
[
25
]
), that for a vector
X
,

\displaystyle\mid\partial_{X}|B|\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\partial_{X}B|\;.

(10.24)

Since
\Sigma_{t=0}
is diffeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, for each
x\in\Sigma_{0}
, there exists
\Omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
, such that
x=r\cdot\Omega
.

Case
q\geq 0
:

Then, we have
q=|x|-t\geq 0
and the point
(t,x)
is therefore outside the outgoing null cone whose tip is the origin. We then apply the fundamental theorem of calculus by integrating at a fixed
\Omega
, from
(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)
along the line
(\tau,r\cdot\Omega)
such that
r+\tau=|x|+t
till we reach the hyperplane
\tau=0
. We obtain,

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A\big(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega\big)\mid+\int_{t+|x|}^{|x|}\partial_{r}\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)\mid dr

(10.25)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A\big(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega\big)\mid+|\int_{t+|x|}^{|x|}\partial_{r}\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)\mid dr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A\big(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega\big)\mid+\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}| \partial_{r}\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)\mid|dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A\big(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega\big)\mid+\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}\mid\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))\mid dr

\displaystyle\text{(see \eqref{absolutevalueofderivativeofabsolutevaluelessthanabsolutofderiva})}.

On one hand, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{r}=\frac{x^{i}}{r}\partial_{i}\;.

(10.26)

and thus

\displaystyle\mid\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t+|x|-r,\mid r\mid\cdot\Omega))\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))\mid\;,

and on the other hand, for
q\geq 0
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(\tau,r\cdot\Omega)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+\tau+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}}\;.

Hence,

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t+|x|-r,\mid r\mid\cdot\Omega)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|-r+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{1+\gamma}}

with
q=r-\tau=r-(t+|x|-r)=2r-t-|x|=|q|
since
q\geq 0
. This means

1+t+|x|-r+|q|=1+t+|x|-r+2r-t-|x|=1+r.

This leads to

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+r)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+2r-t-|x|)^{1+\gamma}}.

Since we integrate in the direction
|x|\leq r
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\frac{\epsilon}{(1+r)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+r-t)^{1+\gamma}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+r-t)^{1+\gamma}},

and therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)|dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+r-t)^{1+\gamma}}dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\big[\frac{-1}{\gamma(1+r-t)^{\gamma}}\big]^{t+|x|}_{|x|}\;.

Hence,

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega)|dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\big(\frac{-1}{\gamma(1+|x|)^{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{\gamma(1+|x|-t)^{\gamma}}\big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\cdot\frac{1}{\gamma(1+|x|-t)^{\gamma}}\;.

However, since at the point
(t,x)
, we have
q=|x|-t=|q|
in this region, and therefore,
|x|=t+|q|
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}|\partial({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A)(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))|dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)}{\gamma}\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}}\;.

From what we have previously proved for
t=0
, for
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-1\}
, we have for the other term on
\tau=0
, the following estimate

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}\;.

Finally, with
q=|x|-t=|q|
, we have
t+|x|=2t+|x|-t=2t+|q|
and hence

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+2t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+2t+|q|)^{\gamma}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}}.

Consequently,

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon

\displaystyle\cdot\big(\frac{1}{\gamma(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}}+\frac{1}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}}\big)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}}.

Identically, we get the same estimate for
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(t,x)
, and hence, for
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-1\}
, for
q\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid+\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\gamma}}\;.

Case
q<0
:

Then, we have
q=|x|-t<0
and the point
(t,x)
is therefore inside the outgoing null cone whose tip is at the origin. We then apply the fundamental theorem of calculus by integrating at a fixed
\Omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
, from
(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)
along the line
(\tau,r\cdot\Omega)
such that
r+\tau=|x|+t
till we reach the hyperplane
\tau=0
. We have for all
\mu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\big(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega\big)\mid+\mid\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid,

and we know that for
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-1\}
,

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}.

In fact

\displaystyle 1+t+|x|\sim 1+t+|q|\;.

(10.29)

Consequently, we obtain

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}},

and thus, we are left out with treating the integral
\mid\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid
.

The line
\tau+r=t+|x|
intersects the outgoing light cone at
\tau=r
, and thus, the intersection point is at
r=\frac{t+\mid x\mid}{2}=\tau
. Computing for any
\mu\in(t,x^{1},\ldots,x^{n})
,

\displaystyle|\int_{|x|}^{t+|x|}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr|+|\int_{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}^{t+|x|}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr|\;.

Treating the second term, which is an integral on a segment in the region
q\geq 0
, i.e.
r\geq t
:

\displaystyle|\int_{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}^{t+|x|}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)-{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\big(\frac{t+|x|}{2},(\frac{t+|x|}{2})\cdot\Omega\big)|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)|+|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}\big(\frac{t+|x|}{2},(\frac{t+|x|}{2})\cdot\Omega\big)|\;.

From what we had proven, we have that the first term, for
\gamma>\max\{0,\delta-1\}
, has the following estimate

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(0,(t+|x|)\cdot\Omega)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}+\gamma-\delta}}\;.

For the second term, since it is on
q=0
, we can use the estimate that we established for
q\geq 0
by plugging in it
q=0
and taking
\tau=\frac{t+|x|}{2}
, to obtain

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(\frac{t+|x|}{2},(\frac{t+|x|}{2})\cdot\Omega)|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+\frac{t+|x|}{2})^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\;.

Thus,

\displaystyle\mid\int_{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}^{t+|x|}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle c(\gamma)\cdot C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\;.

Now, we are left to treat the integral on the region
q<0
, that we can estimate using the estimate on the gradient that we already established, (
10.1
),

\displaystyle\mid\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mid\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\partial(Z^{I}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|-r+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}dr\;.

In this region of integration, we have
q=r-\tau<0
, and thus we have
|q|=-q=\tau-r
. However, we are integrating along the line
r+\tau=t+|x|
and thus,
\tau=t+|x|-r
. Consequently,
|q|=t+|x|-2r
and
q=2r-t-|x|
. Hence,

\displaystyle\mid\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+2t+2|x|-3r)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)}{(1+2t+2|x|-\frac{3(t+|x|)}{2})^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\frac{\epsilon}{(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}}dr

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)}{(1+\frac{(t+|x|)}{2})^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\frac{\epsilon}{(1-q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}dr

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)}{(1+t+|x|))^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\int_{|x|-t}^{0}\frac{\epsilon}{(1-q)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\frac{dq}{2}\;,

where we made the change of variable
q=2r-t-|x|
with
dq=2dr
. We get

\displaystyle\mid\int_{|x|}^{\frac{t+|x|}{2}}\partial_{r}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A_{\mu}(t+|x|-r,r\cdot\Omega))dr\mid

(10.32)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|))^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\cdot\big[-(1-q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big]_{|x|-t}^{0}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|))^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\cdot\big[-1+\big(1-(|x|-t)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+t+|x|))^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\cdot\big(1-(|x|-t)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|))^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}\cdot\big(1+|q|)\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\;.

Putting it all together, we obtain that, for
q<0
, we have the following estimate, using the fact that the same argument works also for
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}
,

\displaystyle\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}A(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid+\mid{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}h^{1}(t,|x|\cdot\Omega)\mid

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle C(|I|)\cdot E(|I|+\lfloor\frac{n}{2}\rfloor+1)\cdot\frac{\epsilon}{(1+t+|q|)^{\frac{(n-1)}{2}-\delta}}(1+|q|)^{\frac{1}{2}}\;.

Thus, we get the result.

We would like now to estimate
|{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{K}}g^{\lambda\mu}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\lambda}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\mu}A_{\cal U}(t,x)|
for
|K|\leq|J|
.

Lemma 10.5
.

The Minkowski covariant derivative commutes with the Lie derivative along Minkowski vector fields, that is for any tensor
K
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}K={\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}K).

(10.34)

Since
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}K
is also a tensor, it follows that
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}
commutes with any product of
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}
.

Note that the Lie derivatives are not being differentiated in
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}({\mathcal{L}}_{Z^{I}}K)
; the differentiation concerns only the tensor
K
.

Proof.

In fact, for simplicity, consider
K
a tensor of order one,
K_{\alpha}
. Let
Z\in\cal Z
. We have

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}K_{\beta}=Z({\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}K_{\beta})-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}e_{\alpha}}K_{\beta}-{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}K_{{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}e_{\beta}}\;.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}K_{\beta}
is a 2-tensor, we can compute it in wave coordinates
\{x^{0},x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}\}
, and if the result we get is also a tensor in
{\alpha},{\beta}
, it would then hold true for any vectors. Let
{\alpha},{\beta}\in\{x^{0},x^{1},\ldots,x^{n}\}
: we know by then that
{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{e_{\alpha}}e_{\beta}=0
and therefore we have

\displaystyle{\nabla^{(\bf{m})}}_{\alpha}K_{\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}K_{\beta}.

(10.35)

We also have
{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}e_{\alpha}=[Z,e_{\alpha}]
. Since
Z
is a Minkowski vector field, it is either a coordinate vector field (and therefore
[Z,e_{\alpha}]=0
) or it is a rotation or a Lorentz boost and can be written as

\displaystyle Z_{\mu\nu}=x_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}-x_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\,

or it is a scaling vector field and can be written as

\displaystyle S=\sum_{\mu=0}^{3}x^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\,.

We have for spatial indices
i,j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, that
e_{\alpha}(x_{i})=e_{\alpha}(x^{i})=\delta_{{\alpha}i}
, and thus, for rotations,

\displaystyle[Z_{ij},e_{\alpha}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[x_{j}\partial_{i}-x_{i}\partial_{j},\partial_{\alpha}]=\delta_{{\alpha}j}\partial_{i}-\delta_{{\alpha}i}\partial_{j}\,.

(10.36)

We have for the Lorentz boosts,

\displaystyle[Z_{0j},e_{\alpha}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[x_{j}\partial_{t}-x_{0}\partial_{j},\partial_{\alpha}]=[x^{j}\partial_{t}+t\partial_{j},\partial_{\alpha}]=\delta_{{\alpha}j}\partial_{t}+\delta_{{\alpha}0}\partial_{j}\,.

(10.37)

For the scaling vector field, we have

\displaystyle[S,e_{\alpha}]

\displaystyle=
